Report issue for preceding element
(November 2023)
Abstract
Report issue for preceding element
Generative Artificial Intelligence (GenAI) systems are increasingly being deployed across diverse industries and research domains. Developers and end-users interact with these systems through the use of prompting and prompt engineering. Although prompt engineering is a widely adopted and extensively researched area, it suffers from conflicting terminology and a fragmented ontological understanding of what constitutes an effective prompt due to its relatively recent emergence. We establish a structured understanding of prompt engineering by assembling a taxonomy of prompting techniques and analyzing their applications. We present a detailed vocabulary of 33 vocabulary terms, a taxonomy of 58 LLM prompting techniques, and 40 techniques for other modalities. Additionally, we provide best practices and guidelines for prompt engineering, including advice for prompting engineering ChatGPT and other state-of-the-art (SOTA) LLMs. We further present a meta-analysis of the entire literature on natural language prefix-prompting. As a culmination of these efforts, this paper presents the most comprehensive survey on prompt engineering to date.
Report issue for preceding element

1 Introduction
Report issue for preceding element
Transformer-based LLMs are widely deployed in consumer-facing, internal, and research settings Bommasani et al. ( 
2021
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib21
) . Typically, these models rely on the user providing an input “prompt” to which the model produces an output in response. Such prompts may be textual—“Write a poem about trees.”—or take other forms: images, audio, videos, or a combination thereof. The ability to prompt models, particularly prompting with natural language, makes them easy to interact with and use flexibly across a wide range of use cases.
Report issue for preceding element
Knowing how to effectively structure, evaluate, and perform other tasks with prompts is essential to using these models. Empirically, better prompts lead to improved results across a wide range of tasks Wei et al. ( 
2022b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib315
); Liu et al. ( 
2023b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib166
); Schulhoff ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib257
) . A large body of literature has grown around the use of prompting to improve results and the number of prompting techniques is rapidly increasing.
Report issue for preceding element
However, as prompting is an emerging field, the use of prompts continues to be poorly understood, with only a fraction of existing terminologies and techniques being well-known among practitioners. We perform a large-scale review of prompting techniques to create a robust resource of terminology and techniques in the field. We expect this to be the first iteration of terminologies that will develop over time. We maintain an up-to-date list of terms and techniques at 
LearnPrompting.org
https://learnprompting.org/
.
Report issue for preceding element
Scope of Study
Report issue for preceding element
We create a broad directory of prompting techniques, that can be quickly understood and easily implemented for rapid experimentation by developers and researchers. To this end, we limit our study to focus on prefix prompts Shin et al. ( 
2020a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib268
) rather than cloze prompts Petroni et al. ( 
2019
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib220
); Cui et al. ( 
2021
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib45
) , because modern LLM transformer architectures widely employ prefix prompts and provide robust support for both developers and researchers Brown et al. ( 
2020
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib25
); Google ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib81
); Touvron et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib290
) . Additionally, we refined our focus to hard (discrete) prompts rather than soft (continuous) prompts and leave out papers that make use of techniques using gradient-based updates (i.e. fine-tuning). Hard prompts contain only tokens (vectors) that correspond to words in the model's vocabulary, while soft prompts may contain tokens that have no corresponding word in the vocabulary.
Report issue for preceding element
Finally, we only study task-agnostic techniques. These decisions keep the work approachable to less technical readers and maintain a manageable scope.
Report issue for preceding element
Sections Overview
Report issue for preceding element
We conducted a machine-assisted systematic review grounded in the PRISMA process Page et al. ( 
2021
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib207
) (Section 
2.1
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch2.S1
) to identify 58 different text-based prompting techniques, from which we create a taxonomy with a robust terminology of prompting terms (Section 
1.2
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch1.S2
).
Report issue for preceding element
 
Figure 1.1: Categories within the field of prompting are interconnected. We discuss 7 core categories that are well described by the papers within our scope. Report issue for preceding element
Our goal is to provide a roadmap for the community when considering which prompting techniques to use (Figure 
1.1
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch1.F1
). While much literature on prompting focuses on English-only settings, we also discuss multilingual techniques (Section 
3.1
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch3.S1
). Given the rapid growth in multimodal prompting, where prompts may include media such as images, we also expand our scope to multimodal techniques (Section 
3.2
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch3.S2
). Many multilingual and multimodal prompting techniques are direct extensions of English text-only prompting techniques.
Report issue for preceding element
As prompting techniques grow more complex, they have begun to incorporate external tools, such as Internet browsing and calculators. We use the term "agents" to describe these types of prompting techniques (Section 
4.1
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch4.S1
).
Report issue for preceding element
It is important to understand how to evaluate the outputs of agents and prompting techniques to ensure accuracy and avoid hallucinations. Thus, we discuss ways of evaluating these outputs (Section 
4.2
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch4.S2
). We also discuss security (Section 
5.1
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch5.S1
) and safety measures (Section 
5.2
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch5.S2
) for designing prompts that reduce the risk of harm to companies and users.
Report issue for preceding element
Finally, we apply prompting techniques in two case studies (Section 
6.1
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch6.S1
). In the first, we test a range of prompting techniques against the commonly used benchmark MMLU Hendrycks et al. ( 
2021
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib94
) . In the second, we explore in detail an example of manual prompt engineering on a significant, real-world use case, identifying signals of frantic hopelessness–a top indicator of suicidal crisis–in the text of individuals seeking support Schuck et al. ( 
2019a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib255
) . We conclude with a discussion of the nature of prompting and its recent development (Section 
8
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch8
).
Report issue for preceding element
1.1 What is a Prompt?
Report issue for preceding element
A prompt is an input to a Generative AI model, that is used to guide its output Meskó ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib189
); White et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib321
); Heston and Khun ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib97
); Hadi et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib88
); Brown et al. ( 
2020
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib25
) . Prompts may consist of text, image, sound, or other media. Some examples of prompts include the text, “write a three paragraph email for a marketing campaign for an accounting firm”, a photograph of a piece of paper with the words “what is 10*179” written on it, or a recording of an online meeting, with the instructions “summarize this”. Prompts usually have some text component, but this may change as non-text modalities become more common.
Report issue for preceding element
Prompt Template
Report issue for preceding element
Prompts are often constructed via a prompt template Shin et al. ( 
2020b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib269
) . A prompt template is a function that contains one or more variables which will be replaced by some media (usually text) to create a prompt. This prompt can then be considered to be an instance of the template.
Report issue for preceding element
Consider applying prompting to the task of binary classification of tweets. Here is an initial prompt template that can be used to classify inputs.
Report issue for preceding element
Classify the tweet as positive or negative: {TWEET} Report issue for preceding element
Each tweet in the dataset would be inserted into a separate instance of the template and the resulting prompt would be given to a LLM for inference.
Report issue for preceding element
Write a poem about trees. Report issue for preceding element
Write a poem about the following topic: {USER_INPUT} Report issue for preceding element
Figure 1.2: Prompts and prompt templates are distinct concepts; a prompt template becomes a prompt when input is inserted into it. Report issue for preceding element

1.2.1 Components of a Prompt
Report issue for preceding element
There are a variety of common components included in a prompt. We summarize the most commonly used components and discuss how they fit into prompts (Figure 
1.3
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch1.F3
).
Report issue for preceding element
Directive
Report issue for preceding element
Many prompts issue a directive in the form of an instruction or question. 
1
 
1
 1“Directives”, from Searle ( 
1969
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib263
) , are a type of speech act intended to encourage an action, and have been invoked in models of human-computer dialogue Morelli et al. ( 
1991
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib194
) . This is the core intent of the prompt, sometimes simply called the "intent". For example, here is an instance of a prompt with a single instruction:
Report issue for preceding element
Tell me five good books to read. Report issue for preceding element
Directives can also be implicit, as in this one-shot case, where the directive is to perform English to Spanish translation:
Report issue for preceding element
Night: Noche Morning: Report issue for preceding element
Examples
Report issue for preceding element
Examples, also known as exemplars or shots, act as demonstrations that guide the GenAI to accomplish a task. The above prompt is a One-Shot (i.e. one example) prompt.
Report issue for preceding element
Output Formatting
Report issue for preceding element
It is often desirable for the GenAI to output information in certain formats, for example, CSV, Markdown, XML, or even custom formats Xia et al. ( 
2024
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib330
) . Structuring outputs may reduce performance on some tasks Tam et al. ( 
2024
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib283
) . However, Kurt ( 
2024
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib135
) point out various flaws in Tam et al. ( 
2024
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib283
) and show that structuring outputs may actually improve performance. Here is an example of how you might format a prompt to output information as a CSV:
Report issue for preceding element
{PARAGRAPH} Summarize this into a CSV. Report issue for preceding element
Style Instructions
Report issue for preceding element
Style instructions are a type of output formatting used to modify the output stylistically rather than structurally (Section 
2.2.1.3
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch2.S2.SS1.SSS3.Px2
). For example:
Report issue for preceding element
Write a clear and curt paragraph about llamas. Report issue for preceding element
Role
Report issue for preceding element
A Role, also known as a persona Schmidt et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib254
); Wang et al. ( 
2023l
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib313
) , is a frequently discussed component that can improve writing and style text (Section 
2.2.1.3
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch2.S2.SS1.SSS3.Px1
). For example:
Report issue for preceding element
Pretend you are a shepherd and write a limerick about llamas. Report issue for preceding element
Additional Information
Report issue for preceding element
It is often necessary to include additional information in the prompt. For example, if the directive is to write an email, you might include information such as your name and position so the GenAI can properly sign the email. Additional Information is sometimes called 'context', though we discourage the use of this term as it is overloaded with other meanings in the prompting space 
2
 
2
 2 e.g. the context is the tokens processed by the LLM in a forward pass.
Report issue for preceding element
1.2.2 Prompting Terms
Report issue for preceding element
Terminology within the prompting literature is rapidly developing. As it stands, there are many poorly understood definitions (e.g. prompt, prompt engineering) and conflicting ones (e.g. role prompt vs persona prompt). The lack of a consistent vocabulary hampers the community's ability to clearly describe the various prompting techniques in use. We provide a robust vocabulary of terms used in the prompting community (Figure 
1.3
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch1.F3
). 
3
 
3
 3 By robust, we mean that it covers most existing commonly used terms in the field. Less frequent terms are left to Appendix 
A.2
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#A1.S2
. In order to accurately define frequently-used terms like prompt and prompt engineering, we integrate many definitions (Appendix 
A.1
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#A1.S1
) to derive representative definitions.
Report issue for preceding element
Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
Prompting is the process of providing a prompt to a GenAI, which then generates a response. For example, the action of sending a chunk of text or uploading an image constitutes prompting.
Report issue for preceding element
Prompt Chain
Report issue for preceding element
A prompt chain (activity: prompt chaining) consists of two or more prompt templates used in succession. The output of the prompt generated by the first prompt template is used to parameterize the second template, continuing until all templates are exhausted Wu et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib328
) .
Report issue for preceding element
Prompting Technique
Report issue for preceding element
A prompting technique is a blueprint that describes how to structure a prompt, prompts, or dynamic sequencing of multiple prompts. A prompting technique may incorporate conditional or branching logic, parallelism, or other architectural considerations spanning multiple prompts.
Report issue for preceding element
Prompt Engineering
Report issue for preceding element
Prompt engineering is the iterative process of developing a prompt by modifying or changing the prompting technique that you are using (Figure 
1.4
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch1.F4
).
Report issue for preceding element
 
Figure 1.4: The Prompt Engineering Process consists of three repeated steps 1) performing inference on a dataset 2) evaluating performance and 3) modifying the prompt template. Note that the extractor is used to extract a final response from the LLM output (e.g. "This phrase is positive" → → \rightarrow → "positive"). See more information on extractors in Section 
2.5
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch2.S5
. Report issue for preceding element
Prompt Engineering Technique
Report issue for preceding element
A prompt engineering technique is a strategy for iterating on a prompt to improve it. In literature, this will often be automated techniques Deng et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib48
) , but in consumer settings, users often perform prompt engineering manually, without any assistive tooling.
Report issue for preceding element
Exemplar
Report issue for preceding element
Exemplars are examples of a task being completed that are shown to a model in a prompt Brown et al. ( 
2020
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib25
) .
Report issue for preceding element
1.3 A Short History of Prompts
Report issue for preceding element
The idea of using natural language prefixes, or prompts, to elicit language model behaviors and responses originated before the GPT-3 and ChatGPT era. GPT-2 Radford et al. ( 
2019a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib236
) makes use of prompts and they appear to be first used in the context of Generative AI by Fan et al. ( 
2018
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib58
) . However, the concept of prompts was preceded by related concepts such as control codes Pfaff ( 
1979
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib222
); Poplack ( 
1980
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib225
); Keskar et al. ( 
2019
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib118
) and writing prompts in literature.
Report issue for preceding element
The term Prompt Engineering appears to have come into existence more recently from Radford et al. ( 
2021
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib235
) then slightly later from Reynolds and McDonell ( 
2021
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib241
) .
Report issue for preceding element
However, various papers perform prompt engineering without naming the term Wallace et al. ( 
2019
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib296
); Shin et al. ( 
2020a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib268
) , including Schick and Schütze ( 
2020a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib251
, 
b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib252
); Gao et al. ( 
2021
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib72
) for non-autoregressive language models.
Report issue for preceding element
Some of the first works on prompting define a prompt slightly differently to how it is currently used. For example, consider the following prompt from Brown et al. ( 
2020
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib25
) :
Report issue for preceding element
Translate English to French: llama Report issue for preceding element
Brown et al. ( 
2020
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib25
) consider the word "llama" to be the prompt, while "Translate English to French:" is the "task description". More recent papers, including this one, refer to the entire string passed to the LLM as the prompt.
Report issue for preceding element
2 A Meta-Analysis of Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
2.1 Systematic Review Process
Report issue for preceding element
In order to robustly collect a dataset of sources for this paper, we ran a systematic literature review grounded in the PRISMA process Page et al. ( 
2021
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib207
) (Figure 
2.1
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch2.F1
). We host this dataset on HuggingFace 
4
 
4
 4 https://huggingface.co/datasets/PromptSystematicReview/Prompt_Systematic_Review_Dataset and present a datasheet Gebru et al. ( 
2021
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib76
) for the dataset in Appendix 
A.3
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#A1.S3
. Our main data sources were 
arXiv
https://arxiv.org/
, 
Semantic Scholar
https://semanticscholar.com/
, and 
ACL
https://aclanthology.org/
. We query these databases with a list of 44 keywords narrowly related to prompting and prompt engineering (Appendix 
A.4
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#A1.S4
).
Report issue for preceding element
2.1.1 The Pipeline
Report issue for preceding element
In this section, we introduce our data scraping pipeline, which includes both human and LLM-assisted review. 
5
 
5
 5 Using gpt-4-1106-preview As an initial sample to establish filtering critera, we retrieve papers from arXiv based on a simple set of keywords and boolean rules ( 
A.4
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#A1.S4
). Then, human annotators label a sample of 1,661 articles from the arXiv set for the following criteria:
Report issue for preceding element
Include if the paper proposes a novel prompting technique. Report issue for preceding element
Include if the paper strictly covers hard prefix prompts. Report issue for preceding element
Exclude if the paper focuses on training by backpropagating gradients. Report issue for preceding element
Include if the paper uses a masked frame and/or window for non-text modalities. Report issue for preceding element
A set of 300 articles are reviewed independently by two annotators, with 92% agreement (Krippendorff's α 𝛼 \alpha italic_α = Cohen's κ 𝜅 \kappa italic_κ = 81%). Next, we develop a prompt using gpt-4-1106-preview to classify the remaining articles (Appendix 
A.5
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#A1.S5
). We validate the prompt against 100 ground-truth annotations, achieving 89% precision and 75% recall (for an F  1 𝐹 1 F1 italic_F 1 of 81%). The combined human and LLM annotations generate a final set of 1,565 papers.
Report issue for preceding element
 
Figure 2.1: The PRISMA systematic literature review process. We accumulate 4,247 unique records from which we extract 1,565 relevant records. Report issue for preceding element
2.2 Text-Based Techniques
Report issue for preceding element
We now present a comprehensive taxonomical ontology of 58 text-based prompting techniques, broken into 6 major categories (Figure 
2.2
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch2.F2
). Although some of the techniques might fit into multiple categories, we place them in a single category of most relevance.

2.2.1 In-Context Learning (ICL)
Report issue for preceding element
 
Figure 2.3: We highlight six main design decisions when crafting few-shot prompts. 
∗
 Please note that recommendations here do not generalize to all tasks; in some cases, each of them could hurt performance. Report issue for preceding element
ICL refers to the ability of GenAIs to learn skills and tasks by providing them with exemplars and or relevant instructions within the prompt, without the need for weight updates/retraining Brown et al. ( 
2020
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib25
); Radford et al. ( 
2019b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib237
) . These skills can be learned from exemplars (Figure 
2.4
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch2.F4
) and/or instructions (Figure 
2.5
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch2.F5
). Note that the word "learn" is misleading. ICL can simply be task specification–the skills are not necessarily new, and can have already been included in the training data (Figure 
2.6
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch2.F6
). See Appendix 
A.9
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#A1.S9
 for a discussion of the use of this term. Significant work is currently being done on optimizing Bansal et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib17
) and understanding Si et al. ( 
2023a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib272
); Štefánik and Kadlčík ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib281
) ICL.
Report issue for preceding element
2+2: four 4+5: nine 8+0: Report issue for preceding element Figure 2.4: ICL exemplar prompt Report issue for preceding element Extract all words that have 3 of the same letter and at least 3 other letters from the following text: {TEXT} Report issue for preceding element Figure 2.5: ICL instruction prompt Report issue for preceding element Translate the word "cheese" to French. Report issue for preceding element Figure 2.6: ICL from training data prompt. In this version of ICL, the model is not learning a new skill, but rather using knowledge likely in its training set. Report issue for preceding element
Few-Shot Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
Brown et al. ( 
2020
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib25
) is the paradigm seen in Figure 
2.4
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch2.F4
, where the GenAI learns to complete a task with only a few examples (exemplars). Few-shot prompting is a special case of Few-Shot Learning (FSL) Fei-Fei et al. ( 
2006
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib59
); Wang et al. ( 
2019
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib309
) , but does not require updating of model parameters
Report issue for preceding element
2.2.1.1 Few-Shot Prompting Design Decisions
Report issue for preceding element
Selecting exemplars for a prompt is a difficult task–performance depends significantly on various factors of the exemplars Dong et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib53
) , and only a limited number of exemplars fit in the typical LLM's context window. We highlight six separate design decisions, including the selection and order of exemplars that critically influence the output quality Zhao et al. ( 
2021a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib361
); Lu et al. ( 
2021
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib178
); Ye and Durrett ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib346
) (Figure 
2.3
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch2.F3
).
Report issue for preceding element
Exemplar Quantity
Report issue for preceding element
Increasing the quantity of exemplars in the prompt generally improves model performance, particularly in larger models Brown et al. ( 
2020
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib25
) . However, in some cases, the benefits may diminish beyond 20 exemplars Liu et al. ( 
2021
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib164
) . In the case of long context LLMs, additional exemplars continue to increase performance, though efficiency varies depending on task and model Agarwal et al. ( 
2024
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib2
); Bertsch et al. ( 
2024
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib19
); Jiang et al. ( 
2024
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib110
) .
Report issue for preceding element
Exemplar Ordering
Report issue for preceding element
The order of exemplars affects model behavior Lu et al. ( 
2021
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib178
); Kumar and Talukdar ( 
2021
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib134
); Liu et al. ( 
2021
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib164
); Rubin et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib245
) . On some tasks, exemplar order can cause accuracy to vary from sub-50% to 90%+ Lu et al. ( 
2021
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib178
) .
Report issue for preceding element
Exemplar Label Distribution
Report issue for preceding element
As in traditional supervised machine learning, the distribution of exemplar labels in the prompt affects behavior. For example, if 10 exemplars from one class and 2 exemplars of another class are included, this may cause the model to be biased toward the first class.
Report issue for preceding element
Exemplar Label Quality
Report issue for preceding element
Despite the general benefit of multiple exemplars, the necessity of strictly 
valid
 demonstrations is unclear. Some work Min et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib192
) suggests that the accuracy of labels is irrelevant—providing models with exemplars with incorrect labels may not negatively diminish performance. However, under certain settings, there is a significant impact on performance Yoo et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib347
) . Larger models are often better at handling incorrect or unrelated labels Wei et al. ( 
2023c
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib318
) .
Report issue for preceding element
It is important to discuss this factor, since if you are automatically constructing prompts from large datasets that may contain inaccuracies, it may be necessary to study how label quality affects your results.
Report issue for preceding element
Exemplar Format
Report issue for preceding element
The formatting of exemplars also affects performance. One of the most common formats is “Q: {input}, A: {label}”, but the optimal format may vary across tasks; it may be worth trying multiple formats to see which performs best. There is some evidence to suggest that formats that occur commonly in the training data will lead to better performance Jiang et al. ( 
2020
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib112
) .
Report issue for preceding element
Exemplar Similarity
Report issue for preceding element
Selecting exemplars that are similar to the test sample is generally beneficial for performance Liu et al. ( 
2021
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib164
); Min et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib192
) . However, in some cases, selecting more diverse exemplars can improve performance Su et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib282
); Min et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib192
) .
Report issue for preceding element
Instruction Selection
Report issue for preceding element
While instructions are required to guide LLMs in zero-shot prompts (Wei et al., 
2022a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib314
) , the benefits of adding instructions before exemplars in few-shot prompts is less clear. Ajith et al. ( 
2024
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib6
) show that generic, task-agnostic instructions (i.e., no instruction or “Complete the following task:”) improve classification and question answering accuracy over task-specific ones (e.g., What is the answer to this question?) concluding instruction-following abilities can be achieved via exemplars alone. While they may not improve correctness, instructions in few-shot prompts can still guide auxiliary output attributes like writing style (Roy et al., 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib244
) .
Report issue for preceding element
2.2.1.2 Few-Shot Prompting Techniques
Report issue for preceding element
Considering all of these factors, Few-Shot Prompting can be very difficult to implement effectively. We now examine techniques for Few-Shot Prompting in the supervised setting. Ensembling approaches can also benefit Few-Shot Prompting, but we discuss them separately (Section 
2.2.4
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch2.S2.SS4
).
Report issue for preceding element

{x^{i}} italic_D start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT italic_t italic_r italic_a italic_i italic_n end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_x start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT italic_i end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT end_POSTSUBSCRIPT and outputs D y i t  r  a  i  n subscript superscript 𝐷 𝑡 𝑟 𝑎 𝑖 𝑛 superscript 𝑦 𝑖 D^{train}

Here is the prompt template we will use for this section, following the 'input: output' format (Figure 
2.4
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch2.F4

Report issue for preceding element
{Exemplars} D x i t  e  s  t subscript superscript 𝐷 𝑡 𝑒 𝑠 𝑡 superscript 𝑥 𝑖 D^{test}_{x^{i}} italic_D start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT italic_t italic_e italic_s italic_t end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_x start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT italic_i end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT end_POSTSUBSCRIPT : Report issue for preceding element Figure 2.7: Few-Shot Prompting Template Report issue for preceding element
K-Nearest Neighbor (KNN)
Report issue for preceding element
Liu et al. ( 
2021
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib164
) is part of a family of algorithms that selects exemplars similar to D x i t  e  s  t subscript superscript 𝐷 𝑡 𝑒 𝑠 𝑡 superscript 𝑥 𝑖 D^{test}_{x^{i}} italic_D start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT italic_t italic_e italic_s italic_t end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_x start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT italic_i end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT end_POSTSUBSCRIPT to boost performance. Although effective, employing KNN during prompt generation may be time and resource intensive.
Report issue for preceding element
Vote-K
Report issue for preceding element
Su et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib282
) is another method to select similar exemplars to the test sample. In one stage, a model proposes useful unlabeled candidate exemplars for an annotator to label. In the second stage, the labeled pool is used for Few-Shot Prompting. Vote-K also ensures that newly added exemplars are sufficiently different than existing ones to increase diversity and representativeness.
Report issue for preceding element
Self-Generated In-Context Learning (SG-ICL)
Report issue for preceding element
Kim et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib127
) leverages a GenAI to automatically generate exemplars. While better than zero-shot scenarios when training data is unavailable, the generated samples are not as effective as actual data.
Report issue for preceding element
Prompt Mining
Report issue for preceding element
Jiang et al. ( 
2020
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib112
) is the process of discovering optimal "middle words" in prompts through large corpus analysis. These middle words are effectively prompt templates. For example, instead of using the common "Q: A:" format for few-shot prompts, there may exist something similar that occurs more frequently in the corpus. Formats which occur more often in the corpus will likely lead to improved prompt performance.
Report issue for preceding element
More Complicated Techniques
Report issue for preceding element
such as LENS Li and Qiu ( 
2023a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib152
) , UDR Li et al. ( 
2023f
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib151
) , and Active Example Selection Zhang et al. ( 
2022a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib356
) leverage iterative filtering, embedding and retrieval, and reinforcement learning, respectively.
Report issue for preceding element
2.2.1.3 Zero-Shot Prompting Techniques
Report issue for preceding element
In contrast to Few-Shot Prompting, Zero-Shot Prompting uses zero exemplars. There are a number of well-known standalone zero-shot techniques as well as zero-shot techniques combined with another concept (e.g. Chain of Thought), which we discuss later (Section 
2.2.2
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch2.S2.SS2
).
Report issue for preceding element
Role Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
Wang et al. ( 
2023j
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib311
); Zheng et al. ( 
2023d
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib368
) , also known as persona prompting Schmidt et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib254
); Wang et al. ( 
2023l
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib313
) , assigns a specific role to the GenAI in the prompt. For example, the user might prompt it to act like "Madonna" or a "travel writer". This can create more desirable outputs for open-ended tasks Reynolds and McDonell ( 
2021
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib241
) and in some cases may improve accuracy on benchmarks Zheng et al. ( 
2023d
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib368
) .
Report issue for preceding element
Style Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
Lu et al. ( 
2023a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib175
) involves specifying the desired style, tone, or genre in the prompt to shape the output of a GenAI. A similar effect can be achieved using role prompting.
Report issue for preceding element
Emotion Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
Li et al. ( 
2023a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib145
) incorporates phrases of psychological relevance to humans (e.g., "This is important to my career") into the prompt, which may lead to improved LLM performance on benchmarks and open-ended text generation.
Report issue for preceding element
System 2 Attention (S2A)
Report issue for preceding element
Weston and Sukhbaatar ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib320
) first asks an LLM to rewrite the prompt and remove any information unrelated to the question therein. Then, it passes this new prompt into an LLM to retrieve a final response.
Report issue for preceding element
SimToM
Report issue for preceding element
Wilf et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib322
) deals with complicated questions which involve multiple people or objects. Given the question, it attempts to establish the set of facts one person knows, then answer the question based only on those facts. This is a two prompt process and can help eliminate the effect of irrelevant information in the prompt.
Report issue for preceding element
Rephrase and Respond (RaR)
Report issue for preceding element
Deng et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib49
) instructs the LLM to rephrase and expand the question before generating the final answer. For example, it might add the following phrase to the question: "Rephrase and expand the question, and respond". This could all be done in a single pass or the new question could be passed to the LLM separately. RaR has demonstrated improvements on multiple benchmarks.
Report issue for preceding element
Re-reading (RE2)
Report issue for preceding element
Xu et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib333
) adds the phrase "Read the question again:" to the prompt in addition to repeating the question. Although this is such a simple technique, it has shown improvement in reasoning benchmarks, especially with complex questions.
Report issue for preceding element
Self-Ask
Report issue for preceding element
Press et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib228
) prompts LLMs to first decide if they need to ask follow up questions for a given prompt. If so, the LLM generates these questions, then answers them and finally answers the original question.
Report issue for preceding element
2.2.2 Thought Generation
Report issue for preceding element
Thought generation encompasses a range of techniques that prompt the LLM to articulate its reasoning while solving a problem Zhang et al. ( 
2023c
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib357
) .
Report issue for preceding element
Chain-of-Thought (CoT) Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
Wei et al. ( 
2022b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib315
) leverages few-shot prompting to encourage the LLM to express its thought process before delivering its final answer. 
6
 
6
 6 We note that such techniques are often described using words like ”think” that anthropomorphize models. We attempt not to use this language, but do use original authors' language where appropriate. This technique is occasionally referred to as Chain-of-Thoughts Tutunov et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib293
); Besta et al. ( 
2024
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib20
); Chen et al. ( 
2023d
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib35
) . It has been demonstrated to significantly enhance the LLM's performance in mathematics and reasoning tasks. In Wei et al. ( 
2022b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib315
) , the prompt includes an exemplar featuring a question, a reasoning path, and the correct answer (Figure 
2.8
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch2.F8
).
Report issue for preceding element
Q: Jack has two baskets, each containing three balls. How many balls does Jack have in total? A: One basket contains 3 balls, so two baskets contain 3 * 2 = 6 balls. Q: {QUESTION} A: Report issue for preceding element Figure 2.8: A One-Shot Chain-of-Thought Prompt. Report issue for preceding element
2.2.2.1 Zero-Shot-CoT
Report issue for preceding element
The most straightforward version of CoT contains zero exemplars. It involves appending a thought inducing phrase like "Let's think step by step." Kojima et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib133
) to the prompt. Other suggested thought-generating phrases include "First, let's think about this logically" Kojima et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib133
) . Zhou et al. ( 
2022b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib370
) uses LLMs to generate "Let's work this out in a step by step way to be sure we have the right answer". Yang et al. ( 
2023a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib335
) searches for an optimal thought inducer. Zero-Shot-CoT approaches are attractive as they don't require exemplars and are generally task agnostic.
Report issue for preceding element
Step-Back Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
Zheng et al. ( 
2023c
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib367
) is a modification of CoT where the LLM is first asked a generic, high-level question about relevant concepts or facts before delving into reasoning. This approach has improved performance significantly on multiple reasoning benchmarks for both PaLM-2L and GPT-4.
Report issue for preceding element
Analogical Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
Yasunaga et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib344
) is similar to SG-ICL, and automatically generates exemplars that include CoTs. It has demonstrated improvements in mathematical reasoning and code generation tasks.
Report issue for preceding element
Thread-of-Thought (ThoT) Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
Zhou et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib371
) consists of an improved thought inducer for CoT reasoning. Instead of "Let's think step by step," it uses "Walk me through this context in manageable parts step by step, summarizing and analyzing as we go." This thought inducer works well in question-answering and retrieval settings, especially when dealing with large, complex contexts.
Report issue for preceding element
Tabular Chain-of-Thought (Tab-CoT)
Report issue for preceding element
Jin and Lu ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib114
) consists of a Zero-Shot CoT prompt that makes the LLM output reasoning as a markdown table. This tabular design enables the LLM to improve the structure and thus the reasoning of its output.
Report issue for preceding element
2.2.2.2 Few-Shot CoT
Report issue for preceding element
This set of techniques presents the LLM with multiple exemplars, which include chains-of-thought. This can significantly enhance performance. This technique is occasionally referred to as Manual-CoT Zhang et al. ( 
2022b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib358
) or Golden CoT Del and Fishel ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib47
) .
Report issue for preceding element
Contrastive CoT Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
Chia et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib40
) adds both exemplars with incorrect and correct explanations to the CoT prompt in order to show the LLM how not to reason. This method has shown significant improvement in areas like Arithmetic Reasoning and Factual QA.
Report issue for preceding element
Uncertainty-Routed CoT Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
Google ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib81
) samples multiple CoT reasoning paths, then selects the majority if it is above a certain threshold (calculated based on validation data). If not, it samples greedily and selects that response. This method demonstrates improvement on the MMLU benchmark for both GPT-4 and Gemini Ultra models.
Report issue for preceding element
Complexity-based Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
Fu et al. ( 
2023b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib65
) involves two major modifications to CoT. First, it selects complex examples for annotation and inclusion in the prompt, based on factors like question length or reasoning steps required. Second, during inference, it samples multiple reasoning chains (answers) and uses a majority vote among chains exceeding a certain length threshold, under the premise that longer reasoning indicates higher answer quality. This technique has shown improvements on three mathematical reasoning datasets.
Report issue for preceding element
Active Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
Diao et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib51
) starts with some training questions/exemplars, asks the LLM to solve them, then calculates uncertainty (disagreement in this case) and asks human annotators to rewrite the exemplars with highest uncertainty.
Report issue for preceding element
Memory-of-Thought Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
Li and Qiu ( 
2023b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib153
) leverage unlabeled training exemplars to build Few-Shot CoT prompts at test time. Before test time, it performs inference on the unlabeled training exemplars with CoT. At test time, it retrieves similar instances to the test sample. This technique has shown substantial improvements in benchmarks like Arithmetic, commonsense, and factual reasoning.
Report issue for preceding element
Automatic Chain-of-Thought (Auto-CoT) Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
Zhang et al. ( 
2022b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib358
) uses Wei et al. ( 
2022b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib315
) 's Zero-Shot prompt to automatically generate chains of thought. These are then used to build a Few-Shot CoT prompt for a test sample.
Report issue for preceding element
2.2.3 Decomposition
Report issue for preceding element
Significant research has focused on decomposing complex problems into simpler sub-questions. This is an effective problem-solving strategy for humans as well as GenAI Patel et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib209
) . Some decomposition techniques are similar to thought-inducing techniques, such as CoT, which often naturally breaks down problems into simpler components. However, explicitly breaking down problems can further improve LLMs' problem solving ability.
Report issue for preceding element
Least-to-Most Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
Zhou et al. ( 
2022a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib369
) starts by prompting a LLM to break a given problem into sub-problems without solving them. Then, it solves them sequentially, appending model responses to the prompt each time, until it arrives at a final result. This method has shown significant improvements in tasks involving symbolic manipulation, compositional generalization, and mathematical reasoning.
Report issue for preceding element
Decomposed Prompting (DECOMP)
Report issue for preceding element
Khot et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib124
) Few-Shot prompts a LLM to show it how to use certain functions. These might include things like string splitting or internet searching; these are often implemented as separate LLM calls. Given this, the LLM breaks down its original problem into sub-problems which it sends to different functions. It has shown improved performance over Least-to-Most prompting on some tasks.
Report issue for preceding element
Plan-and-Solve Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
Wang et al. ( 
2023f
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib304
) consists of an improved Zero-Shot CoT prompt, "Let's first understand the problem and devise a plan to solve it. Then, let's carry out the plan and solve the problem step by step". This method generates more robust reasoning processes than standard Zero-Shot-CoT on multiple reasoning datasets.
Report issue for preceding element
Tree-of-Thought (ToT)
Report issue for preceding element
Yao et al. ( 
2023b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib341
) , also known as Tree of Thoughts, Long ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib173
) , creates a tree-like search problem by starting with an initial problem then generating multiple possible steps in the form of thoughts (as from a CoT). It evaluates the progress each step makes towards solving the problem (through prompting) and decides which steps to continue with, then keeps creating more thoughts. ToT is particularly effective for tasks that require search and planning.
Report issue for preceding element
Recursion-of-Thought
Report issue for preceding element
Lee and Kim ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib140
) is similar to regular CoT. However, every time it encounters a complicated problem in the middle of its reasoning chain, it sends this problem into another prompt/LLM call. After this is completed, the answer is inserted into the original prompt. In this way, it can recursively solve complex problems, including ones which might otherwise run over that maximum context length. This method has shown improvements on arithmetic and algorithmic tasks. Though implemented using fine-tuning to output a special token that sends sub-problem into another prompt, it could also be done only through prompting.
Report issue for preceding element
Program-of-Thoughts
Report issue for preceding element
Chen et al. ( 
2023d
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib35
) uses LLMs like Codex to generate programming code as reasoning steps. A code interpreter executes these steps to obtain the final answer. It excels in mathematical and programming-related tasks but is less effective for semantic reasoning tasks.
Report issue for preceding element
Faithful Chain-of-Thought
Report issue for preceding element
Lyu et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib183
) generates a CoT that has both natural language and symbolic language (e.g. Python) reasoning, just like Program-of-Thoughts. However, it also makes use of different types of symbolic languages in a task-dependent fashion.
Report issue for preceding element
Skeleton-of-Thought
Report issue for preceding element
Ning et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib203
) focuses on accelerating answer speed through parallelization. Given a problem, it prompts an LLM to create a skeleton of the answer, in a sense, sub-problems to be solved. Then, in parallel, it sends these questions to a LLM and concatenates all the outputs to get a final response.
Report issue for preceding element
Metacognitive Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
Wang and Zhao ( 
2024
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib310
) attempts to make the LLM mirror human metacognitive processes with a five part prompt chain, with steps including clarifying the question, preliminary judgement, evaluation of response, decision confirmation, and confidence assessment.
Report issue for preceding element
2.2.4 Ensembling
Report issue for preceding element
In GenAI, ensembling is the process of using multiple prompts to solve the same problem, then aggregating these responses into a final output. In many cases, a majority vote—selecting the most frequent response—is used to generate the final output. Ensembling techniques reduce the variance of LLM outputs and often improving accuracy, but come with the cost of increasing the number of model calls needed to reach a final answer.
Report issue for preceding element
Demonstration Ensembling (DENSE)
Report issue for preceding element
Khalifa et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib120
) creates multiple few-shot prompts, each containing a distinct subset of exemplars from the training set. Next, it aggregates over their outputs to generate a final response.
Report issue for preceding element
Mixture of Reasoning Experts (MoRE)
Report issue for preceding element
Si et al. ( 
2023d
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib275
) creates a set of diverse reasoning experts by using different specialized prompts for different reasoning types (such as retrieval augmentation prompts for factual reasoning, Chain-of-Thought reasoning for multi-hop and math reasoning, and generated knowledge prompting for commonsense reasoning). The best answer from all experts is selected based on an agreement score.
Report issue for preceding element
Max Mutual Information Method
Report issue for preceding element
Sorensen et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib279
) creates multiple prompt templates with varied styles and exemplars, then selects the optimal template as the one that maximizes mutual information between the prompt and the LLM's outputs.
Report issue for preceding element
Self-Consistency
Report issue for preceding element
Wang et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib307
) is based on the intuition that multiple different reasoning paths can lead to the same answer. This method first prompts the LLM multiple times to perform CoT, crucially with a non-zero temperature to elicit diverse reasoning paths. Next, it uses a majority vote over all generated responses to select a final response. Self-Consistency has shown improvements on arithmetic, commonsense, and symbolic reasoning tasks.
Report issue for preceding element
Universal Self-Consistency
Report issue for preceding element
Chen et al. ( 
2023e
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib36
) is similar to Self-Consistency except that rather than selecting the majority response by programmatically counting how often it occurs, it inserts all outputs into a prompt template that selects the majority answer. This is helpful for free-form text generation and cases where the same answer may be output slightly differently by different prompts.
Report issue for preceding element
Meta-Reasoning over Multiple CoTs
Report issue for preceding element
Yoran et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib348
) is similar to universal Self-Consistency; it first generates multiple reasoning chains (but not necessarily final answers) for a given problem. Next, it inserts all of these chains in a single prompt template then generates a final answer from them.
Report issue for preceding element
DiVeRSe
Report issue for preceding element
Li et al. ( 
2023i
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib157
) creates multiple prompts for a given problem then performs Self-Consistency for each, generating multiple reasoning paths. They score reasoning paths based on each step in them then select a final response.
Report issue for preceding element
Consistency-based Self-adaptive Prompting (COSP)
Report issue for preceding element
Wan et al. ( 
2023a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib297
) constructs Few-Shot CoT prompts by running Zero-Shot CoT with Self-Consistency on a set of examples then selecting a high agreement subset of the outputs to be included in the final prompt as exemplars. It again performs Self-Consistency with this final prompt.
Report issue for preceding element
Universal Self-Adaptive Prompting (USP)
Report issue for preceding element
Wan et al. ( 
2023b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib298
) builds upon the success of COSP, aiming to make it generalizable to all tasks. USP makes use of unlabeled data to generate exemplars and a more complicated scoring function to select them. Additionally, USP does not use Self-Consistency.
Report issue for preceding element
Prompt Paraphrasing
Report issue for preceding element
Jiang et al. ( 
2020
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib112
) transforms an original prompt by changing some of the wording, while still maintaining the overall meaning. It is effectively a data augmentation technique that can be used to generate prompts for an ensemble.
Report issue for preceding element
2.2.5 Self-Criticism
Report issue for preceding element
When creating GenAI systems, it can be useful to have LLMs criticize their own outputs (Huang et al., 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib103
) . This could simply be a judgement (e.g., is this output correct) or the LLM could be prompted to provide feedback, which is then used to improve the answer. Many approaches to generating and integrating self-criticism have been developed.
Report issue for preceding element
Self-Calibration
Report issue for preceding element
Kadavath et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib115
) first prompts an LLM to answer a question. Then, it builds a new prompt that includes the question, the LLM's answer, and an additional instruction asking whether the answer is correct. This can be useful for gauging confidence levels when applying LLMs when deciding when to accept or revise the original answer.
Report issue for preceding element
Self-Refine
Report issue for preceding element
Madaan et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib185
) is an iterative framework where, given an initial answer from the LLM, it prompts the same LLM to provide feedback on the answer, and then prompts the LLM to improve the answer based on the feedback. This iterative process continues until a stopping condition is met (e.g., max number of steps reached). Self-Refine has demonstrated improvement across a range of reasoning, coding, and generation tasks.
Report issue for preceding element
Reversing Chain-of-Thought (RCoT)
Report issue for preceding element
Xue et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib334
) first prompts LLMs to reconstruct the problem based on generated answer. Then, it generates fine-grained comparisons between the original problem and the reconstructed problem as a way to check for any inconsistencies. These inconsistencies are then converted to feedback for the LLM to revise the generated answer.
Report issue for preceding element
Self-Verification
Report issue for preceding element
Weng et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib319
) generates multiple candidate solutions with Chain-of-Thought (CoT). It then scores each solution by masking certain parts of the original question and asking an LLM to predict them based on the rest of the question and the generated solution. This method has shown improvement on eight reasoning datasets.
Report issue for preceding element
Chain-of-Verification (COVE)
Report issue for preceding element
Dhuliawala et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib50
) first uses an LLM to generate an answer to a given question. Then, it creates a list of related questions that would help verify the correctness of the answer. Each question is answered by the LLM, then all the information is given to the LLM to produce the final revised answer. This method has shown improvements in various question-answering and text-generation tasks.
Report issue for preceding element
Cumulative Reasoning
Report issue for preceding element
Zhang et al. ( 
2023b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib355
) first generates several potential steps in answering the question. It then has a LLM evaluate them, deciding to either accept or reject these steps. Finally, it checks whether it has arrived at the final answer. If so, it terminates the process, but otherwise it repeats it. This method has demonstrated improvements in logical inference tasks and mathematical problem.
Report issue for preceding element
2.3 Prompting Technique Usage
Report issue for preceding element
 
Figure 2.9: Citation Counts of GenAI Models
 
Figure 2.10: Citation Counts of Datasets Report issue for preceding element
 
Figure 2.11: Citation Counts of Prompting Techniques. The top 25 papers in our dataset, measured by how often they are cited by other papers in our dataset. Most papers here are prompting techniques*, and the remaining papers contains prompting advice. Report issue for preceding element
Report issue for preceding element
As we have just seen, there exist many text-based prompting techniques. However, only a small subset of them are commonly used in research and in industry. We measure technique usage by proxy of measuring the number of citations by other papers in our dataset. We do so with the presumption that papers about prompting are more likely to actually use or evaluate the cited technique. We graph the top 25 papers cited in this way from our dataset and find that most of them propose new prompting techniques (Figure 
2.11
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch2.F11
). The prevalence of citations for Few-Shot and Chain-of-Thought prompting is unsurprising and helps to establish a baseline for understanding the prevalence of other techniques.
Report issue for preceding element
2.3.1 Benchmarks
Report issue for preceding element
In prompting research, when researchers propose a new technique, they usually benchmark it across multiple models and datasets. This is important to prove the utility of the technique and examine how it transfers across models.
Report issue for preceding element
In order to make it easier for researchers proposing new techniques to know how to benchmark them, we quantitatively examine which models (Figure 
2.11
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch2.F11
) and what benchmark datasets (Figure 
2.11
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch2.F11
) are being used. Again, we measure usage by how many times papers in our dataset cite the benchmark datasets and models.
Report issue for preceding element
To find which datasets and models are being used, we prompted GPT-4-1106-preview to extract any mentioned dataset or model from the body of papers in our dataset. After, we manually filtered out results that were not models or datasets. The citation counts were acquired by searching items from the finalized list on Semantic Scholar.
Report issue for preceding element
2.4 Prompt Engineering
Report issue for preceding element
In addition to surveying prompting techniques, we also review prompt engineering techniques, which are used to automatically optimize prompts. We discuss some techniques that use gradient updates, since the set of prompt engineering techniques is much smaller than that of prompting techniques.
Report issue for preceding element
Meta Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
is the process of prompting a LLM to generate or improve a prompt or prompt template Reynolds and McDonell ( 
2021
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib241
); Zhou et al. ( 
2022b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib370
); Ye et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib345
) . This is often done without any scoring mechanism, using just a simple template (Figure 
2.12
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch2.F12
). However, other works present more complex uses of meta-prompting, with multiple iterations and scoring mechanisms Yang et al. ( 
2023a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib335
); Fernando et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib62
) .
Report issue for preceding element
Improve the following prompt: {PROMPT} Report issue for preceding element Figure 2.12: A simple Meta Prompting template. Report issue for preceding element
AutoPrompt
Report issue for preceding element
Shin et al. ( 
2020b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib269
) uses a frozen LLM as well as a prompt template that includes some "trigger tokens", whose values are updated via backpropogation at training time. This is a version of soft-prompting.
Report issue for preceding element
Automatic Prompt Engineer (APE)
Report issue for preceding element
Zhou et al. ( 
2022b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib370
) uses a set of exemplars to generate a Zero-Shot instruction prompt. It generates multiple possible prompts, scores them, then creates variations of the best ones (e.g. by using prompt paraphrasing). It iterates on this process until some desiderata are reached.
Report issue for preceding element
Gradientfree Instructional Prompt Search (GrIPS)
Report issue for preceding element
Prasad et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib226
) is similar to APE, but uses a more complex set of operations including deletion, addition, swapping, and paraphrasing in order to create variations of a starting prompt.
Report issue for preceding element
Prompt Optimization with Textual Gradients (ProTeGi)
Report issue for preceding element
Pryzant et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib229
) is a unique approach to prompt engineering that improves a prompt template through a multi-step process. First, it passes a batch of inputs through the template, then passes the output, ground truth, and prompt into another prompt that criticizes the original prompt. It generates new prompts from these criticisms then uses a bandit algorithm Gabillon et al. ( 
2011
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib66
) to select one. ProTeGi demonstrates improvements over methods like APE and GRIPS.
Report issue for preceding element
RLPrompt
Report issue for preceding element
Deng et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib48
) uses a frozen LLM with an unfrozen module added. It uses this LLM to generate prompt templates, scores the templates on a dataset, and updates the unfrozen module using Soft Q-Learning Guo et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib85
) . Interestingly, the method often selects grammatically nonsensical text as the optimal prompt template.
Report issue for preceding element
Dialogue-comprised Policy-gradient-based Discrete Prompt Optimization (DP2O)
Report issue for preceding element
Li et al. ( 
2023b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib146
) is perhaps the most complicated prompt engineering technique, involving reinforcement learning, a custom prompt scoring function, and conversations with an LLM to construct the prompt.
Report issue for preceding element
2.5 Answer Engineering
Report issue for preceding element
Answer engineering is the iterative process of developing or selecting among algorithms that extract precise answers from LLM outputs. To understand the need for answer engineering, consider a binary classification task where the labels are "Hate Speech" and "Not Hate Speech". The prompt template might look like this:
Report issue for preceding element
Is this "Hate Speech" or "Not Hate Speech": {TEXT} Report issue for preceding element
When a hate speech sample is put through the template, it might have outputs such as "It's hate speech", "Hate Speech.", or even "Hate speech, because it uses negative language against a racial group". This variance in response formats is difficult to parse consistently; improved prompting can help, but only to a certain extent.
Report issue for preceding element
There are three design decisions in answer engineering, the choice of answer space, answer shape, and answer extractor (Figure 
2.13
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch2.F13
). Liu et al. ( 
2023b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib166
) define the first two as necessary components of answer engineering and we append the third. We consider answer engineering to be distinct from prompt engineering, but extremely closely related; the processes are often conducted in tandem.
Report issue for preceding element
 
Figure 2.13: An annotated output of a LLM output for a labeling task, which shows the three design decisions of answer engineering: the choice of answer shape, space, and extractor. Since this is an output from a classification task, the answer shape could be restricted to a single token and the answer space to one of two tokens ("positive" or "negative"), though they are unrestricted in this image. Report issue for preceding element
2.5.1 Answer Shape
Report issue for preceding element
The shape of an answer is its physical format. For example, it could be a token, span of tokens, or even an image or video. 
7
 
7
 7 We use a different definition than Liu et al. ( 
2023b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib166
) with respect to granularity (e.g. token vs span), since the output could be of a different modality. It is sometimes useful to restrict the output shape of a LLM to a single token for tasks like binary classification.
Report issue for preceding element
2.5.2 Answer Space
Report issue for preceding element
The space of an answer is the domain of values that its structure may contain. This may simply be the space of all tokens, or in a binary labeling task, could just be two possible tokens.
Report issue for preceding element
2.5.3 Answer Extractor
Report issue for preceding element
In cases where it is impossible to entirely control the answer space (e.g. consumer-facing LLMs), or the expected answer may be located somewhere within the model output, a rule can be defined to extract the final answer. This rule is often a simple function (e.g. a regular expression), but can also use a separate LLM to extract the answer.
Report issue for preceding element
Verbalizer
Report issue for preceding element
Often used in labeling tasks, a verbalizer maps a token, span, or other type of output to a label and vice-versa (injective) Schick and Schütze ( 
2021
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib253
) . For example, if we wish for a model to predict whether a Tweet is positive or negative, we could prompt it to output either "+" or "-" and a verbalizer would map these token sequences to the appropriate labels. The selection of a verbalizer constitutes a component of answer engineering.
Report issue for preceding element
Regex
Report issue for preceding element
As mentioned previously, Regexes are often used to extract answers. They are usually used to search for the first instance of a label. However, depending on the output format and whether CoTs are generated, it may be better to search for the last instance.
Report issue for preceding element
Separate LLM
Report issue for preceding element
Sometimes outputs are so complicated that regexes won't work consistently. In this case, it can be useful to have a separate LLM evaluate the output and extract an answer. This separate LLM will often use an answer trigger Kojima et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib133
) , e.g. "The answer (Yes or No) is", to extract the answer.
Report issue for preceding element
3 Beyond English Text Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
Prompting GenAIs with English text currently stands as the dominant method for interaction. Prompting in other languages or through different modalities often requires special techniques to achieve comparable performance. In this context, we discuss the domains of multilingual and multimodal prompting.
Report issue for preceding element
