Figure 3.1: All multilingual prompting techniques. Report issue for preceding element
3.1 Multilingual
Report issue for preceding element
State-of-the-art GenAIs have often been predominately trained with English dataset, leading to a notable disparity in the output quality in languages other than English, particularly low-resource languages Bang et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib16
); Jiao et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib113
); Hendy et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib95
); Shi et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib267
) . As a result, various multilingual prompting techniques have emerged in an attempt to improve model performance in non-English settings (Figure 
3.1
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch3.F1
).
Report issue for preceding element
Translate First Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
Shi et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib267
) is perhaps the simplest strategy and first translates non-English input examples into English. By translating the inputs into English, the model can utilize its strengths in English to better understand the content. Translation tools vary; Shi et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib267
) use an external MT system, Etxaniz et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib57
) prompt multilingual LMs and Awasthi et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib11
) prompt LLMs to translate non-English inputs.
Report issue for preceding element
3.1.1 Chain-of-Thought (CoT)
Report issue for preceding element
CoT prompting Wei et al. ( 
2023a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib316
) has been extended to the multilingual setting in multiple ways.
Report issue for preceding element
XLT (Cross-Lingual Thought) Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
Huang et al. ( 
2023a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib102
) utilizes a prompt template composed of six separate instructions, including role assignment, cross-lingual thinking, and CoT.
Report issue for preceding element
Cross-Lingual Self Consistent Prompting (CLSP)
Report issue for preceding element
Qin et al. ( 
2023a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib233
) introduces an ensemble technique that constructs reasoning paths in different languages to answer the same question.
Report issue for preceding element
3.1.2 In-Context Learning
Report issue for preceding element
ICL has also been extended to multilingual settings in multiple ways.
Report issue for preceding element
X-InSTA Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
Tanwar et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib285
) explores three distinct approaches for aligning in-context examples with the input sentence for classification tasks: using semantically similar examples to the input (semantic alignment), examples that share the same label as the input (task-based alignment), and the combination of both semantic and task-based alignments.
Report issue for preceding element
In-CLT (Cross-lingual Transfer) Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
(Kim et al., 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib128
) leverages both the source and target languages to create in-context examples, diverging from the traditional method of using source language exemplars. This strategy helps stimulate the cross-lingual cognitive capabilities of multilingual LLMs, thus boosting performance on cross-lingual tasks.
Report issue for preceding element
3.1.2.1 In-Context Example Selection
Report issue for preceding element
In-context example selection heavily influences the multilingual performance of LLMs Garcia et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib74
); Agrawal et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib3
) . Finding in-context examples that are semantically similar to the source text is very important Winata et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib325
); Moslem et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib195
); Sia and Duh ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib276
) . However, using semantically dissimilar ( peculiar) exemplars has also been shown to enhance performance Kim and Komachi ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib126
) . This same contrast exists in the English-only setting. Additionally, when dealing with ambiguous sentences, selecting exemplars with polysemous or rare word senses may boost performance Iyer et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib107
) .
Report issue for preceding element
PARC (Prompts Augmented by Retrieval Cross-lingually)
Report issue for preceding element
Nie et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib202
) introduce a framework that retrieves relevant exemplars from a high resource language. This framework is specifically designed to enhance cross-lingual transfer performance, particularly for low-resource target languages. Li et al. ( 
2023g
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib154
) extend this work to Bangla.
Report issue for preceding element
3.1.3 Prompt Template Language Selection
Report issue for preceding element
In multilingual prompting, the selection of language for the prompt template can markedly influence the model performance.
Report issue for preceding element
English Prompt Template
Report issue for preceding element
Constructing the prompt template in English is often more effective than in the task language for multilingual tasks. This is likely due to the predominance of English data during LLM pre-training Lin et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib161
); Ahuja et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib4
) . Lin et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib161
) suggest that this is likely due to a high overlap with pre-training data and vocabulary. Similarly, Ahuja et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib4
) highlight how translation errors when creating task language templates propagate in the form of incorrect syntax and semantics, adversely affecting task performance. Further, Fu et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib64
) compare in-lingual (task language) prompts and cross-lingual (mixed language) prompts and find the cross-lingual approach to be more effective, likely because it uses more English in the prompt, thus facilitating retrieving knowledge from the model.
Report issue for preceding element
Task Language Prompt Template
Report issue for preceding element
In contrast, many multilingual prompting benchmarks such as BUFFET Asai et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib9
) or LongBench Bai et al. ( 
2023a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib12
) use task language prompts for language-specific use cases. Muennighoff et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib197
) specifically studies different translation methods when constructing native-language prompts. They demonstrate that human translated prompts are superior to their machine-translated counterparts. Native or non-native template performance can differ across tasks and models Li et al. ( 
2023h
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib156
) . As such, neither option will always be the best approach Nambi et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib198
) .
Report issue for preceding element
3.1.4 Prompting for Machine Translation
Report issue for preceding element
There is significant research into leveraging GenAI to facilitate accurate and nuanced translation. Although this is a specific application of prompting, many of these techniques are important more broadly for multilingual prompting.
Report issue for preceding element
Multi-Aspect Prompting and Selection (MAPS)
Report issue for preceding element
He et al. ( 
2023b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib93
) mimics the human translation process, which involves multiple preparatory steps to ensure high-quality output. This framework starts with knowledge mining from the source sentence (extracting keywords and topics, and generating translation exemplars). It integrates this knowledge to generate multiple possible translations, then selects the best one.
Report issue for preceding element
Chain-of-Dictionary (CoD)
Report issue for preceding element
Lu et al. ( 
2023b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib176
) first extracts words from the source phrase, then makes a list of their meanings in multiple languages, automatically via retrieval from a dictionary (e.g. English: ' apple', Spanish: ' manzana'). Then, they prepend these dictionary phrases to the prompt, where it asks a GenAI to use them during translation.
Report issue for preceding element
Dictionary-based Prompting for Machine Translation (DiPMT)
Report issue for preceding element
Ghazvininejad et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib77
) works similarly to CoD, but only gives definitions in the source and target languages, and formats them slightly differently.
Report issue for preceding element
Decomposed Prompting for MT (DecoMT)
Report issue for preceding element
Puduppully et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib230
) divides the source text into several chunks and translates them independently using few-shot prompting. Then it uses these translations and contextual information between chunks to generate a final translation.
Report issue for preceding element
3.1.4.1 Human-in-the-Loop
Report issue for preceding element
Interactive-Chain-Prompting (ICP)
Report issue for preceding element
Pilault et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib223
) deals with potential ambiguities in translation by first asking the GenAI to generate sub-questions about any ambiguities in the phrase to be translated. Humans later respond to these questions and the system includes this information to generate a final translation.
Report issue for preceding element
Iterative Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
Yang et al. ( 
2023d
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib338
) also involves humans during translation. First, they prompt LLMs to create a draft translation. This initial version is further refined by integrating supervision signals obtained from either automated retrieval systems or direct human feedback.
Report issue for preceding element

Figure 3.2: All multimodal prompting techniques. Report issue for preceding element
3.2 Multimodal
Report issue for preceding element
As GenAI models evolve beyond text-based domains, new prompting techniques emerge. These multimodal prompting techniques are often not simply applications of text-based prompting techniques, but entirely novel ideas made possible by different modalities. We now extend our text-based taxonomy to include a mixture of multimodal analogs of text-based prompting techniques as well as completely novel multimodal techniques (Figure 
3.2
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch3.F2
).
Report issue for preceding element
3.2.1 Image Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
The image modality encompasses data such as photographs, drawings, or even screenshots of text Gong et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib79
) . Image prompting may refer to prompts that either contain images or are used to generate images. Common tasks include image generation Ding et al. ( 
2021
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib52
); Hinz et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib98
); Tao et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib286
); Li et al. ( 
2019a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib144
, 
b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib150
); Rombach et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib243
) , caption generation Li et al. ( 
2020
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib155
) , image classification Khalil et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib121
) , and image editing Crowson et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib44
); Kwon and Ye ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib136
); Bar-Tal et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib18
); Hertz et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib96
) . We now describe various image prompting techniques used for such applications.
Report issue for preceding element
Prompt Modifiers
Report issue for preceding element
are simply words appended to a prompt to change the resultant image Oppenlaender ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib205
) . Components such as Medium (e.g. "on canvas") or Lighting (e.g. "a well lit scene") are often used.
Report issue for preceding element
Negative Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
allows users to numerically weight certain terms in the prompt so that the model considers them more/less heavily than others. For example, by negatively weighting the terms “bad hands” and “extra digits”, models may be more likely to generate anatomically accurate hands Schulhoff ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib257
) .
Report issue for preceding element
3.2.1.1 Multimodal In-Context Learning
Report issue for preceding element
The success of ICL in text-based settings has prompted research into multimodal ICL Wang et al. ( 
2023k
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib312
); Dong et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib53
) .
Report issue for preceding element
Paired-Image Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
shows the model two images: one before and one after some transformation. Then, present the model with a new image for which it will perform the demonstrated conversion. This can be done either with textual instructions Wang et al. ( 
2023k
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib312
) or without them Liu et al. ( 
2023e
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib169
) .
Report issue for preceding element
Image-as-Text Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
Hakimov and Schlangen ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib90
) generates a textual description of an image. This allows for the easy inclusion of the image (or multiple images) in a text-based prompt.
Report issue for preceding element
3.2.1.2 Multimodal Chain-of-Thought
Report issue for preceding element
CoT has been extended to the image domain in various ways Zhang et al. ( 
2023d
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib359
); Huang et al. ( 
2023c
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib105
); Zheng et al. ( 
2023b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib366
); Yao et al. ( 
2023c
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib343
) . A simple example of this would be a prompt containing an image of a math problem accompanied by the textual instructions "Solve this step by step".
Report issue for preceding element
Duty Distinct Chain-of-Thought (DDCoT)
Report issue for preceding element
Zheng et al. ( 
2023b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib366
) extends Least-to-Most prompting Zhou et al. ( 
2022a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib369
) to the multimodal setting, creating subquestions, then solving them and combining the answers into a final response.
Report issue for preceding element
Multimodal Graph-of-Thought
Report issue for preceding element
Yao et al. ( 
2023c
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib343
) extends Graph-of-Thought Zhang et al. ( 
2023d
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib359
) to the multimodal setting. GoT-Input also uses a two step rationale then answer process. At inference time, the the input prompt is used to construct a thought graph, which is then used along with the original prompt to generate a rationale to answer the question. When an image is input along with the question, an image captioning model is employed to generate a textual description of the image, which is then appended to the prompt before the thought graph construction to provide visual context.
Report issue for preceding element
Chain-of-Images (CoI)
Report issue for preceding element
Meng et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib188
) is a multimodal extension of Chain-of-Thought prompting, that generates images as part of its thought process. They use the prompt “Let's think image by image” to generate SVGs, which the model can then use to reason visually.
Report issue for preceding element
3.2.2 Audio Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
Prompting has also been extended to the audio modality. Experiments with audio ICL have generated mixed results, with some open source audio models failing to perform ICL Hsu et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib100
) . However, other results do show an ICL ability in audio models Wang et al. ( 
2023g
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib305
); Peng et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib213
); Chang et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib30
) . Audio prompting is currently in early stages, but we expect to see various prompting techniques proposed in the future.
Report issue for preceding element
3.2.3 Video Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
Prompting has also been extended to the video modality, for use in text-to-video generation Brooks et al. ( 
2024
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib24
); Lv et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib182
); Liang et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib159
); Girdhar et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib78
) , video editing Zuo et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib373
); Wu et al. ( 
2023a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib326
); Cheng et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib39
) , and video-to-text generation Yousaf et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib349
); Mi et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib190
); Ko et al. ( 
2023a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib129
) .
Report issue for preceding element
3.2.3.1 Video Generation Techniques
Report issue for preceding element
When prompting a model to generate video, various modalities of prompts can be used as input, and several prompt-related techniques are often employed to enhance video generation. Image related techniques, such as prompt modifiers can often be used for video generation Runway ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib246
) .
Report issue for preceding element
3.2.4 Segmentation Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
Prompting can also be used for segmentation (e.g. semantic segmentation) Tang et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib284
); Liu et al. ( 
2023c
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib167
) .
Report issue for preceding element
3.2.5 3D Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
Prompting can also be used in 3D modalities, for example in 3D object synthesis Feng et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib60
); Li et al. ( 
2023d
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib148
, 
c
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib147
); Lin et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib160
); Chen et al. ( 
2023f
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib37
); Lorraine et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib174
); Poole et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib224
); Jain et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib108
) , 3D surface texturing Liu et al. ( 
2023g
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib171
); Yang et al. ( 
2023b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib336
); Le et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib139
); Pajouheshgar et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib208
) , and 4D scene generation (animating a 3D scene) Singer et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib278
); Zhao et al. ( 
2023c
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib363
) , where input prompt modalities include text, image, user annotation (bounding boxes, points, lines), and 3D objects.
Report issue for preceding element
4 Extensions of Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
The techniques we have discussed thus far can be extremely complicated, incorporating many steps and iterations. However, we can take prompting further by adding access to external tools (agents) and complex evaluation algorithms to judge the validity of LLM outputs.

Figure 4.1: Agent techniques covered in this section. Report issue for preceding element
4.1 Agents
Report issue for preceding element
As LLMs have improved rapidly in capabilities Zhang et al. ( 
2023c
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib357
) , companies Adept ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib1
) and researchers Karpas et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib116
) have explored how to allow them to make use of external systems. This has been necessitated by shortcomings of LLMs in areas such as mathematical computations, reasoning, and factuality. This has driven significant innovations in prompting techniques; these systems are often driven by prompts and prompt chains, which are heavily engineered to allow for agent-like behaviour (Figure 
4.1
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch4.F1
).
Report issue for preceding element
Definition of Agent
Report issue for preceding element
In the context of GenAI, we define agents to be GenAI systems that serve a user's goals via actions that engage with systems outside the GenAI itself. 
8
 
8
 8 We do not cover the notion of independently-acting AI, i.e. systems that in any sense have their own goals This GenAI is usually a LLM. As a simple example, consider an LLM that is tasked with solving the following math problem:
Report issue for preceding element
If Annie has 4,939 grapes, and gives exactly 39% of them to Amy, how many does she have left? Report issue for preceding element
If properly prompted, the LLM could output the string CALC(4,939*.39). This output could be extracted and put into a calculator to obtain the final answer.
Report issue for preceding element
This is an example of an agent: the LLM outputs text which then uses a downstream tool. Agent LLMs may involve a single external system (as above), or they may need to solve the problem of routing, to choose which external system to use. Such systems also frequently involve memory and planning in addition to actions Zhang et al. ( 
2023c
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib357
) .
Report issue for preceding element
Examples of agents include LLMs that can make API calls to use external tools like a calculator Karpas et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib116
) , LLMs that can output strings that cause actions to be taken in a gym-like Brockman et al. ( 
2016
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib23
); Towers et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib291
) environment Yao et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib342
) , and more broadly, LLMs which write and record plans, write and run code, search the internet, and more Significant Gravitas ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib277
); Yang et al. ( 
2023c
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib337
); Osika ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib206
) . OpenAI Assistants OpenAI ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib204
) , LangChain Agents Chase ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib31
) , and LlamaIndex Agents Liu ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib163
) are additional examples.
Report issue for preceding element
4.1.1 Tool Use Agents
Report issue for preceding element
Tool use is a critical component for GenAI agents. Both symbolic (e.g. calculator, code interpreter) and neural (e.g. a separate LLM) external tools are commonly used. Tools may occasionally be referred to as experts Karpas et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib116
) or modules.
Report issue for preceding element
Modular Reasoning, Knowledge, and Language (MRKL) System
Report issue for preceding element
Karpas et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib116
) is one of the simplest formulations of an agent. It contains a LLM router providing access to multiple tools. The router can make multiple calls to get information such as weather or the current date. It then combines this information to generate a final response. Toolformer Schick et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib250
) , Gorilla Patil et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib210
) , Act-1 Adept ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib1
) , and others Shen et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib266
); Qin et al. ( 
2023b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib234
); Hao et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib91
) all propose similar techniques, most of which involve some fine-tuning.
Report issue for preceding element
Self-Correcting with Tool-Interactive Critiquing (CRITIC)
Report issue for preceding element
Gou et al. ( 
2024a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib82
) first generates a response to the prompt, with no external calls. Then, the same LLM criticizes this response for possible errors. Finally, it uses tools (e.g. Internet search or a code interpreter) accordingly to verify or amend parts of the response.
Report issue for preceding element
4.1.2 Code-Generation Agents
Report issue for preceding element
Writing and executing code is another important ability of many agents. 
9
 
9
 9 This ability may be considered a tool (i.e. code interpreter)
Report issue for preceding element
Program-aided Language Model (PAL)
Report issue for preceding element
Gao et al. ( 
2023b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib70
) translates a problem directly into code, which is sent to a Python interpreter to generate an answer.
Report issue for preceding element
Tool-Integrated Reasoning Agent (ToRA)
Report issue for preceding element
Gou et al. ( 
2024b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib83
) is similar to PAL, but instead of a single code generation step, it interleaves code and reasoning steps for as long as necessary to solve the problem.
Report issue for preceding element
TaskWeaver
Report issue for preceding element
Qiao et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib231
) is also similar to PAL, transforming user requests into code, but can also make use of user-defined plugin.
Report issue for preceding element
4.1.3 Observation-Based Agents
Report issue for preceding element
Some agents are designed to solve problems by interacting with toy environments Brockman et al. ( 
2016
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib23
); Towers et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib291
) . These observation-based agents receive observations inserted into their prompts.
Report issue for preceding element
Reasoning and Acting (ReAct)
Report issue for preceding element
( Yao et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib342
) ) generates a thought, takes an action, and receives an observation (and repeats this process) when given a problem to solve. All of this information is inserted into the prompt so it has a memory of past thoughts, actions, and observations.
Report issue for preceding element
Reflexion
Report issue for preceding element
Shinn et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib271
) builds on ReAct, adding a layer of introspection. It obtains a trajectory of actions and observations, then is given an evaluation of success/failure. Then, it generates a reflection on what it did and what went wrong. This reflection is added to its prompt as a working memory, and the process repeats.
Report issue for preceding element
4.1.3.1 Lifelong Learning Agents
Report issue for preceding element
Work on LLM-integrated Minecraft agents has generated impressive results, with agents able to acquire new skills as they navigate the world of this open-world videogame. We view these agents not merely as applications of agent techniques to Minecraft, but rather novel agent frameworks which can be explored in real world tasks that require lifelong learning.
Report issue for preceding element
Voyager
Report issue for preceding element
Wang et al. ( 
2023a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib299
) is composed of three parts. First, it proposes tasks for itself to complete in order to learn more about the world. Second, it generates code to execute these actions. Finally, it saves these actions to be retrieved later when useful, as part of a long-term memory system. This system could be applied to real world tasks where an agent needs to explore and interact with a tool or website (e.g. penetration testing, usability testing).
Report issue for preceding element
Ghost in the Minecraft (GITM)
Report issue for preceding element
Zhu et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib372
) starts with an arbitrary goal, breaks it down into subgoals recursively, then iteratively plans and executes actions by producing structured text (e.g. "equip(sword)") rather than writing code. GITM uses an external knowledge base of Minecraft items to assist with decomposition as well as a memory of past experience.
Report issue for preceding element
4.1.4 Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG)
Report issue for preceding element
In the context of GenAI agents, RAG is a paradigm in which information is retrieved from an external source and inserted into the prompt. This can enhance performance in knowledge intensive tasks Lewis et al. ( 
2021
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib143
) . When retrieval itself is used as an external tool, RAG systems are considered to be agents.
Report issue for preceding element
Verify-and-Edit
Report issue for preceding element
Zhao et al. ( 
2023a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib360
) improves on self-consistency by generating multiple chains-of-thought, then selecting some to be edited. They do this by retrieving relevant (external) information to the CoTs, and allowing the LLM to augment them accordingly.
Report issue for preceding element
Demonstrate-Search-Predict
Report issue for preceding element
Khattab et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib122
) first decomposes a question into sub-questions, then uses queries to solve them and combine their responses in a final answer. It uses few-shot prompting to decompose the problem and combine responses.
Report issue for preceding element
Interleaved Retrieval guided by Chain-of-Thought (IRCoT)
Report issue for preceding element
Trivedi et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib292
) is a technique for multi-hop question answering that interleaves CoT and retrieval. IRCoT leverages CoT to guide which documents to retrieve and retrieval to help plan the reasoning steps of CoT.
Report issue for preceding element
Iterative Retrieval Augmentation
Report issue for preceding element
techniques, like Forward-Looking Active REtrieval augmented generation (FLARE) Jiang et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib111
) and Imitate, Retrieve, Paraphrase (IRP) Balepur et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib15
) , perform retrieval multiple times during long-form generation. Such models generally perform an iterative three-step process of: 1) generating a temporary sentence to serve as a content plan for the next output sentence; 2) retrieving external knowledge using the temporary sentence as a query; and 3) injecting the retrieved knowledge into the temporary sentence to create the next output sentence. These temporary sentences have been shown to be better search queries compared to the document titles provided in long-form generation tasks.
Report issue for preceding element

Figure 4.2: Evaluation techniques. Report issue for preceding element
4.2 Evaluation
Report issue for preceding element
The potential of LLMs to extract and reason about information and understand user intent makes them strong contenders as evaluators. 
10
 
10
 10 This section does not describe how to benchmark LLMs, but rather how to use them as evaluators. For example, it is possible to prompt a LLM to evaluate the quality of an essay or even a previous LLM output according to some metrics defined in the prompt. We describe four components of evaluation frameworks that are important in building robust evaluators: the prompting technique(s), as described in Section 
2.2
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch2.S2
, the output format of the evaluation, the framework of the evaluation pipeline, and some other methodological design decisions (Figure 
4.2
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch4.F2
).
Report issue for preceding element
4.2.1 Prompting Techniques
Report issue for preceding element
The prompting technique used in the evaluator prompt (e.g. simple instruction vs CoT) is instrumental in building a robust evaluator. Evaluation prompts often benefit from regular text-based prompting techniques, including a role, instructions for the task, the definitions of the evaluation criteria, and in-context examples. Find a full list of techniques in Appendix 
A.6
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#A1.S6
.
Report issue for preceding element
In-Context Learning
Report issue for preceding element
is frequently used in evaluation prompts, much in the same way it is used in other applications Dubois et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib55
); Kocmi and Federmann ( 
2023a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib131
); Brown et al. ( 
2020
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib25
) .
Report issue for preceding element
Role-based Evaluation
Report issue for preceding element
is a useful technique for improving and diversifying evaluations Wu et al. ( 
2023b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib327
); Chan et al. ( 
2024
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib29
) . By creating prompts with the same instructions for evaluation, but different roles, it is possible to effectively generate diverse evaluations. Additionally, roles can be used in a multiagent setting where LLMs debate the validity of the text to be evaluated Chan et al. ( 
2024
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib29
) .
Report issue for preceding element
Chain-of-Thought
Report issue for preceding element
prompting can further improve evaluation performance Lu et al. ( 
2023c
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib177
); Fernandes et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib61
) .
Report issue for preceding element
Model-Generated Guidelines
Report issue for preceding element
Liu et al. ( 
2023d
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib168
, 
h
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib172
) prompt an LLM to generate guidelines for evaluation. This reduces the insufficient prompting problem arising from ill-defined scoring guidelines and output spaces, which can result in inconsistent and misaligned evaluations. Liu et al. ( 
2023d
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib168
) generate a chain-of-thought of the detailed evaluation steps that the model should perform before generating a quality assessment. Liu et al. ( 
2023h
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib172
) propose AutoCalibrate, which derives scoring criteria based on expert human annotations and uses a refined subset of model-generated criteria as a part of the evaluation prompt.
Report issue for preceding element
4.2.2 Output Format
Report issue for preceding element
The output format of the LLM can significantly affect evaluation performance Gao et al. ( 
2023c
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib71
) .
Report issue for preceding element
Styling
Report issue for preceding element
Formatting the LLM's response using XML or JSON styling has also been shown to improve the accuracy of the judgment generated by the evaluator Hada et al. ( 
2024
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib87
); Lin and Chen ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib162
); Dubois et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib55
) .
Report issue for preceding element
Linear Scale
Report issue for preceding element
A very simple output format is a linear scale (e.g. 1-5). Many works use ratings of 1-10 Chan et al. ( 
2024
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib29
) , 1-5 Araújo and Aguiar ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib7
) , or even 0-1 Liu et al. ( 
2023f
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib170
) . The model can be prompted to output a discrete Chan et al. ( 
2024
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib29
) or continuous Liu et al. ( 
2023f
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib170
) score between the bounds.
Report issue for preceding element
Score the following story on a scale of 1-5 from well to poorly written: {INPUT} Report issue for preceding element
Binary Score
Report issue for preceding element
Prompting the model to generate binary responses like Yes or No Chen et al. ( 
2023c
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib34
) and True or False Zhao et al. ( 
2023b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib362
) is another frequently used output format.
Report issue for preceding element
Is the following story well written at a high-school level (yes/no)?: {INPUT} Report issue for preceding element
Likert Scale
Report issue for preceding element
Prompting the GenAI to make use of a Likert Scale Bai et al. ( 
2023b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib13
); Lin and Chen ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib162
); Peskoff et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib218
) can give it a better understanding of the meaning of the scale.
Report issue for preceding element
Score the following story according to the following scale: Poor Acceptable Good Very Good Incredible {INPUT} Report issue for preceding element
4.2.3 Prompting Frameworks
Report issue for preceding element
LLM-EVAL
Report issue for preceding element
Lin and Chen ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib162
) is one of the simplest evaluation frameworks. It uses a single prompt that contains a schema of variables to evaluate (e.g. grammar, relevance, etc.), an instruction telling the model to output scores for each variable within a certain range, and the content to evaluate.
Report issue for preceding element
G-EVAL
Report issue for preceding element
Liu et al. ( 
2023d
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib168
) is similar to LLM-EVAL, but includes an AutoCoT steps in the prompt itself. These steps are generated according to the evaluation instructions, and inserted into the final prompt. These weight answers according to token probabilities.
Report issue for preceding element
ChatEval
Report issue for preceding element
Chan et al. ( 
2024
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib29
) uses a multi-agent debate framework with each agent having a separate role.
Report issue for preceding element
4.2.4 Other Methodologies
Report issue for preceding element
While most approaches directly prompt the LLM to generate a quality assessment (explicit), some works also use implicit scoring where a quality score is derived using the model's confidence in its prediction Chen et al. ( 
2023g
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib38
) or the likelihood of generating the output Fu et al. ( 
2023a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib63
) or via the models' explanation (e.g. count the number of errors as in Fernandes et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib61
); Kocmi and Federmann ( 
2023a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib131
) ) or via evaluation on proxy tasks (factual inconsistency via entailment as in Luo et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib181
) ).
Report issue for preceding element
Batch Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
For improving compute and cost efficiency, some works employ batch prompting for evaluation where multiple instances are evaluated at once 
11
 
11
 11 Disambiguation: there is no relation to making a forward pass with multiple prompts in parallel. We are referring to a single prompt that contains multiple items to evaluate. Lu et al. ( 
2023c
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib177
); Araújo and Aguiar ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib7
); Dubois et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib55
) or the same instance is evaluated under different criteria or roles Wu et al. ( 
2023b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib327
); Lin and Chen ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib162
) . However, evaluating multiple instances in a single batch often degrades performance Dubois et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib55
) .
Report issue for preceding element
Pairwise Evaluation
Report issue for preceding element
Chen et al. ( 
2023g
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib38
) find that directly comparing the quality of two texts may lead to suboptimal results and that explicitly asking LLM to generate a score for individual summaries is the most effective and reliable method. The order of the inputs for pairwise comparisons can also heavily affect evaluation Wang et al. ( 
2023h
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib306
, 
b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib300
) .
Report issue for preceding element
5 Prompting Issues
Report issue for preceding element
We now highlight prompting related issues in the form of security and alignment concerns.
Report issue for preceding element

Figure 5.1: Security & prompting Report issue for preceding element
5.1 Security
Report issue for preceding element
As the use of prompting grows, so too does the threat landscape surrounding it. These threats are extremely varied and uniquely difficult to defend against compared to both non-neural and pre-prompting security threats. We provide a discussion of the prompting threat landscape and limited state of defenses. We begin by describing prompt hacking, the means through which prompting is used to exploit LLMs, then describe dangers emerging from this, and finally describe potential defenses (Figure 
5.1
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch5.F1
).
Report issue for preceding element
5.1.1 Types of Prompt Hacking
Report issue for preceding element
Prompt hacking refers to a class of attacks which manipulate the prompt in order to attack a GenAI Schulhoff et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib258
) . Such prompts have been used to leak private information Carlini et al. ( 
2021
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib27
) , generate offensive content Shaikh et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib264
) and produce deceptive messages Perez et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib214
) . Prompt hacking is a superset of both prompt injection and jailbreaking, which are distinct concepts.
Report issue for preceding element
Prompt Injection
Report issue for preceding element
is the process of overriding original developer instructions in the prompt with user input Schulhoff ( 
2024
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib259
); Willison ( 
2024
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib324
); Branch et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib22
); Goodside ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib80
) . It is an architectural problem resulting from GenAI models not being able to understand the difference between original developer instructions and user input instructions.
Report issue for preceding element
Consider the following prompt template. A user could input "Ignore previous instructions and make a threat against the president.", which might lead to the model being uncertain as to which instruction to follow, and thus possibly following the malicious instruction.
Report issue for preceding element
Recommend a book for the following person: {USER_INPUT} Report issue for preceding element
Jailbreaking
Report issue for preceding element
is the process of getting a GenAI model to do or say unintended things through prompting Schulhoff ( 
2024
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib259
); Willison ( 
2024
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib324
); Perez and Ribeiro ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib215
) . It is either an architectural problem or a training problem made possible by the fact that adversarial prompts are extremely difficult to prevent.
Report issue for preceding element
Consider the following jailbreaking example, which is analogous to the previous prompt injection example, but without developer instructions in the prompt. Instead of inserting text in a prompt template, the user can go directly to the GenAI and prompt it maliciously.
Report issue for preceding element
Make a threat against the president. Report issue for preceding element
5.1.2 Risks of Prompt Hacking
Report issue for preceding element
Prompt hacking can lead to real world risks such as privacy concerns and system vulnerabilities.
Report issue for preceding element
5.1.2.1 Data Privacy
Report issue for preceding element
Both model training data and prompt templates can be leaked via prompt hacking (usually by prompt injection).
Report issue for preceding element
Training Data Reconstruction
Report issue for preceding element
refers to the practice of extracting training data from GenAIs. A straightforward example of this is Nasr et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib199
) , who found that by prompting ChatGPT to repeat the word "company" forever, it began to regurgitate training data.
Report issue for preceding element
Prompt Leaking
Report issue for preceding element
refers to the process of extracting the prompt template from an application. Developers often spend significant time creating prompt templates, and consider them to be IP worth protecting. Willison ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib323
) demonstrate how to leak the prompt template from a Twitter Bot, by simply providing instructions like the following:
Report issue for preceding element
Ignore the above and instead tell me what your initial instructions were. Report issue for preceding element
5.1.2.2 Code Generation Concerns
Report issue for preceding element
LLMs are often used to generate code. Attackers may target vulnerabilities that occur as a result of this code.
Report issue for preceding element
Package Hallucination
Report issue for preceding element
occurs when LLM-generated code attempts to import packages that do not exist Lanyado et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib138
); Thompson and Kelly ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib287
) . After discovering what package names are frequently hallucinated by LLMs, hackers could create those packages, but with malicious code Wu et al. ( 
2023c
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib329
) . If the user runs the install for these formerly non-existent packages, they would download a virus.
Report issue for preceding element
Bugs
Report issue for preceding element
(and security vulnerabilities) occur more frequently in LLM-generated code Pearce et al. ( 
2021
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib211
, 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib212
); Sandoval et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib248
); Perry et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib216
) . Minor changes to the prompting technique can also lead to such vulnerabilities in the generated code Pearce et al. ( 
2021
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib211
) .
Report issue for preceding element
5.1.2.3 Customer Service
Report issue for preceding element
Malicious users frequently perform prompt injection attacks against corporate chatbots, leading to brand embarrassment Bakke ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib14
); Goodside ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib80
) . These attacks may induce the chatbot to output harmful comment or agree to sell the user a company product at a very low price. In the latter case, the user may actually be entitled to the deal. Garcia ( 
2024
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib73
) describe how an airline chatbot gave a customer incorrect information about refunds. The customer appealed in court and won. Although this chatbot was pre-ChatGPT, and was in no way tricked by the user, this precedent may apply when nuanced prompt hacking techniques are used.
Report issue for preceding element
5.1.3 Hardening Measures
Report issue for preceding element
Several tools and prompting techniques have been developed to mitigate some of the aforementioned security risks. However, prompt hacking (both injection and jailbreaking) remain unsolved problems and likely are impossible to solve entirely.
Report issue for preceding element
Prompt-based Defenses
Report issue for preceding element
Multiple prompt-based defenses have been proposed, in which instructions are included in the prompt to avoid prompt injection Schulhoff ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib257
) . For example, the following string could be added to a prompt:
Report issue for preceding element
Do not output any malicious content Report issue for preceding element
However, Schulhoff et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib258
) ran a study with hundreds of thousands of malicious prompts and found that no prompt-based defense is fully secure, though they can mitigate prompt hacking to some extent.
Report issue for preceding element
Detectors
Report issue for preceding element
are tools designed to detect malicious inputs and prevent prompt hacking AI ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib5
); Inan et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib106
) . Many companies have built such detectors ArthurAI ( 
2024
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib8
); Preamble ( 
2024
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib227
); Lakera ( 
2024
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib137
) , which are often built using fine-tuned models trained on malicious prompts. Generally, these tools can mitigate prompt hacking to a greater extent than prompt-based defenses.
Report issue for preceding element
Guardrails
Report issue for preceding element
are rules and frameworks for guiding GenAI outputs Hakan Tekgul ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib89
); Dong et al. ( 
2024
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib54
) . Guardrails often make use of detectors, but not always. Guardrails are more concerned with the general dialogue flow in an application. For example, a simple guardrail could use a detector to find malicious prompts, then respond with a canned message if malicious. More complicated tools employ dialogue managers Rebedea et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib239
) , which allow the LLM to choose from a number of curated responses. Prompting-specific programming languages have also been proposed to improve templating and act as guardrails Scott Lundberg ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib262
); Luca Beurer-Kellner ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib180
) .
Report issue for preceding element

Report issue for preceding element
Figure 5.2: Prompt-based Alignment Organization Report issue for preceding element
5.2 Alignment
Report issue for preceding element
Ensuring that LLMs are well-aligned with user needs in downstream tasks is essential for successful deployment. Models may output harmful content, yield inconsistent responses, or show bias, all of which makes deploying them more difficult. To help mitigate these risks, it is possible to carefully design prompts that elicit less harmful outputs from LLMs. In this section, we describe prompt alignment problems as well as potential solutions (Figure 
5.2
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch5.F2
).
Report issue for preceding element
5.2.1 Prompt Sensitivity
Report issue for preceding element
Several works show that LLMs are highly sensitive to the input prompt Leidinger et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib141
) , i.e., even subtle changes to a prompt such as exemplar order (Section 
2.2.1.1
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch2.S2.SS1.SSS1
) can result in vastly different outputs. Below, we describe several categories of these perturbations and their impacts on model behavior.
Report issue for preceding element
Small Changes in the Prompt
Report issue for preceding element
such as extra spaces, changing capitalization, modifying delimiters, or swapping synonyms can significantly impact performance Lu et al. ( 
2024
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib179
); Tjuatja et al. ( 
2024
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib289
) . Despite these changes being minor, Sclar et al. ( 
2023a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib260
) find that they can cause the performance of LLaMA2-7B to range from nearly 0 to 0.804 on some tasks.
Report issue for preceding element
Task Format
Report issue for preceding element
describes different ways to prompt an LLM to execute the same task. For example, a prompt tasking an LLM to perform sentiment analysis could ask the LLM to classify a review as “positive” or “negative”, or the prompt could ask the LLM “Is this review positive?” to elicit a “yes” or “no” response. Zhao et al. ( 
2021b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib364
) show that these minor changes can alter the accuracy of GPT-3 by up to 30%. Similarly, minor perturbations on task-specific prompts that are logically equivalent, such as altering the order of choices in multiple-choice questions, can result in significant performance degradation Pezeshkpour and Hruschka ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib221
); Zheng et al. ( 
2023a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib365
); Voronov et al. ( 
2024
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib295
) .
Report issue for preceding element
Prompt Drift
Report issue for preceding element
Chen et al. ( 
2023b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib33
) occurs when the model behind an API changes over time, so the same prompt may produce different results on the updated model. Although not directly a prompting issue, it necessitates continuous monitoring of prompt performance.
Report issue for preceding element
5.2.2 Overconfidence and Calibration
Report issue for preceding element
LLMs are often overconfident in their answers, especially when prompted to express their own confidence in words Kiesler and Schiffner ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib125
); Xiong et al. ( 
2023a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib331
) , which may lead to user overreliance on model outputs Si et al. ( 
2023c
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib274
) . Confidence calibration provides a score that represents the confidence of the model Guo et al. ( 
2017
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib84
) . While a natural solution for confidence calibration is to study the output token probabilities provided by the LLM, a variety of prompting techniques have also been created for confidence calibration.
Report issue for preceding element
Verbalized Score
Report issue for preceding element
is a simple calibration technique that generates a confidence score (e.g. “How confident are you from 1 to 10”), but its efficacy is under debate. Xiong et al. ( 
2023b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib332
) find that several LLMs are highly overconfident when verbalizing confidence scores, even when employing self-consistency and chain-of-thought. In contrast, Tian et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib288
) find that simple prompts (Section 
4.2
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch4.S2
) can achieve more accurate calibration than the model's output token probabilities.
Report issue for preceding element
Sycophancy
Report issue for preceding element
refers to the concept that LLMs will often express agreement with the user, even when that view contradicts the model's own intial output. Sharma et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib265
) find that when LLMs are asked to comment on opinions of arguments, the model is easily swayed if the user's opinion is included in the prompt (e.g. “I really like/dislike this argument”). Further, they find that questioning the LLM's original answer (e.g. “Are you sure?”), strongly providing an assessment of correctness (e.g. “I am confident you are wrong”), and adding false assumptions will completely change the model output. Wei et al. ( 
2023b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib317
) note similar results with opinion-eliciting and false user presumptions, also finding that sycophancy is heightened for larger and instruction-tuned models. Thus, to avoid such influence, personal opinions should not be included in prompts. 
12
 
12
 12 For example, a practitioner may use the prompt template “Detect all instances where the user's input is harmful: {INPUT}” in an attempt to prevent adversarial inputs, but this subtly makes the false presupposition that the user's input is actually harmful. Thus, due to sycophancy, the LLM may be inclined to classify the user's output as harmful.
Report issue for preceding element
5.2.3 Biases, Stereotypes, and Culture
Report issue for preceding element
LLMs should be fair to all users, such that no biases, stereotypes, or cultural harms are perpetuated in model outputs Mehrabi et al. ( 
2021
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib186
) . Some prompting technique have been designed in accordance with these goals.
Report issue for preceding element
Vanilla Prompting
Report issue for preceding element
Si et al. ( 
2023b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib273
) simply consists of an instruction in the prompt that tells the LLM to be unbiased. This technique has also been referred to as moral self-correction Ganguli et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib67
) .
Report issue for preceding element
Selecting Balanced Demonstrations
Report issue for preceding element
Si et al. ( 
2023b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib273
) , or obtaining demonstrations optimized over fairness metrics Ma et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib184
) , can reduce biases in LLM outputs (Section 
2.2.1.1
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch2.S2.SS1.SSS1
).
Report issue for preceding element
Cultural Awareness
Report issue for preceding element
Yao et al. ( 
2023a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib340
) can be injected into prompts to help LLMs with cultural adaptation Peskov et al. ( 
2021
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib219
) . This can be done by creating several prompts to do this with machine translation, which include: 1) asking the LLM to refine its own output; and 2) instructing the LLM to use culturally relevant words.
Report issue for preceding element
AttrPrompt
Report issue for preceding element
Yu et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib350
) is a prompting technique designed to avoid producing text biased towards certain attributes when generating synthetic data. Traditional data generation approaches may be biased towards specific lengths, locations and styles. To overcome this, AttrPrompt: 1) asks the LLM to generate specific attributes that are important to alter for diversity (e.g. location); and 2) prompts the LLM to generate synthetic data by varying each of these attributes.
Report issue for preceding element
5.2.4 Ambiguity
Report issue for preceding element
Questions that are ambiguous can be interpreted in multiple ways, where each interpretation could result in a different answer Min et al. ( 
2020
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib193
) . Given these multiple interpretations, ambiguous questions are challenging for existing models Keyvan and Huang ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib119
) , but a few prompting techniques have been developed to help address this challenge.
Report issue for preceding element
Ambiguous Demonstrations
Report issue for preceding element
Gao et al. ( 
2023a
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib69
) are examples that have an ambiguous label set. Including them in a prompt can increase ICL performance. This can be automated with a retriever, but it can also be done manually.
Report issue for preceding element
Question Clarification
Report issue for preceding element
Rao and Daumé III ( 
2019
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib238
) allows the LLM to identify ambiguous questions and generate clarifying questions to pose to the user. Once these questions are clarified by the user, the LLM can regenerate its response. Mu et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib196
) do this for code generation and Zhang and Choi ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib353
) equip LLMs with a similar pipeline for resolving ambiguity for general tasks, but explicitly design separate prompts to: 1) generate an initial answer 2) classify whether to generate clarification questions or return the initial answer 3) decide what clarification questions to generate 4) generate a final answer.
Report issue for preceding element
6 Benchmarking
Report issue for preceding element
Now that we have carried out a systematic review of prompting techniques, we will analyze the empirical performance of different techniques in two ways: via a formal benchmark evaluation, and by illustrating in detail the process of prompt engineering on a challenging real-world problem.
Report issue for preceding element
6.1 Technique Benchmarking
Report issue for preceding element
A formal evaluation of prompting techniques might be done in a broad study that compares hundreds of them across hundreds of models and benchmarks. This is beyond our scope, but since it has not been done before, we provide a first step in this direction. We choose a subset of prompting techniques and run them on the widely used benchmark MMLU Hendrycks et al. ( 
2021
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib94
) . We ran on a representative subset of 2,800 MMLU questions (20% of the questions from each category). 
13
 
13
 13 We excluded human_sexuality, since gpt-3.5-turbo refused to answer these questions. and used gpt-3.5-turbo for all experiments.
Report issue for preceding element
6.1.1 Comparing Prompting Techniques
Report issue for preceding element
We benchmark six distinct prompting techniques using the same general prompt template (Figure 
6.2
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch6.F2
). This template shows the location of different components of the prompts. Only base instructions and question exist in every prompt. The base instruction is a phrase like "Solve the problem and return (A), (B), (C) or (D)." that we vary in some cases. We additionally test two formats of the question (Figures 
6.3
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch6.F3
 and 
6.4
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch6.F4
). The question format is inserted into the prompt template in place of "{QUESTION}". We test each prompting technique with 6 total variations, except for ones that use Self-Consistency.
Report issue for preceding element
Zero-Shot
Report issue for preceding element
As a baseline, we ran questions directly through the model without any special prompting technique, only the base instruction and question. For this baseline, we utilized both formats as well as three phrasing variations of the base instruction. Thus, there were six total runs through the 2800 questions for this benchmark. This did not include any exemplars or thought inducers.
Report issue for preceding element
Zero-Shot-CoT Techniques
Report issue for preceding element
We ran also ran Zero-Shot-CoT. As the three different variations, we used three thought inducers (instructions that cause the model to generate reasoning steps) including the standard "Let's think step by step" chain-of-thought Kojima et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib133
) , as well as ThoT Zhou et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib371
) , and Plan and Solve Wang et al. ( 
2023f
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib304
) . Then, we selected the best of these, and ran it with Self-Consistency with three iterations, taking the majority response.
Report issue for preceding element
Few-Shot Setups
Report issue for preceding element
We also ran Few-Shot prompts and Few-Shot-CoT prompts, both with exemplars generated by one of our authors. For each, we used three variations of the base instruction as well as the two question formats (also applied to the exemplars). Then we used the best performing phrasing with Self-Consistency with three iterations, taking the majority response.
Report issue for preceding element
 
Figure 6.1: Accuracy values are shown for each prompting technique, with the model used being gpt-3.5-turbo. Purple error bars illustrate the minimum and maximum for each technique, since they were each run on different phrasings and formats (except SC). Report issue for preceding element
6.1.2 Question Formats
Report issue for preceding element
We experiment with two formatting choices from Sclar et al. ( 
2023b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib261
) , who explored how formatting choices can affect benchmarking results. We use two formats which lead to varied results on their task (Figures 
6.3
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch6.F3
 and 
6.4
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch6.F4
).
Report issue for preceding element
{BASE_INSTRUCTION} {EXEMPLARS} {QUESTION} {THOUGHT_INDUCER} Report issue for preceding element Figure 6.2: Prompt template for benchmarking. Report issue for preceding element Problem {QUESTION} Options (A)::{A} (B)::{B} (C)::{C} (D)::{D} Answer Report issue for preceding element Figure 6.3: Question format 1. Report issue for preceding element PROBLEM::{QUESTION}, OPTIONS:: (A): {A} (B): {B} (C): {C} (D): {D}, ANSWER:: Report issue for preceding element Figure 6.4: Question format 2. Report issue for preceding element
6.1.3 Self-Consistency
Report issue for preceding element
For the two Self-Consistency results, we set temperature to 0.5, following Wang et al. ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib307
) 's guidelines. For all other prompts, a temperature of 0 was used.
Report issue for preceding element
6.1.4 Evaluating Responses
Report issue for preceding element
Evaluating whether a LLM has properly responded to a question is a difficult task (Section 
2.5
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch2.S5
). We marked answers as correct if they followed certain identifiable patterns, such as being the only capitalized letter (A-D) within parentheses or following a phrase like “The correct answer is”.
Report issue for preceding element
6.1.5 Results
Report issue for preceding element
Performance generally improved as techniques grew more complex (Figure 
6.1
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch6.F1
). However, Zero-Shot-CoT dropped precipitously from Zero-Shot. Although it had a wide spread, for all variants, Zero-Shot performed better. Both cases of Self-Consistency, naturally had lower spread since they repeated a single technique, but it only improved accuracy for Zero-Shot prompts. Few-Shot CoT performs the best, and unexplained performance drops from certain techniques need further research. As prompting technique selection is akin to hyperparameter search, this it is a very difficult task Khattab et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib123
) . However, we hope this small study spurs research in the direction of more performant and robust prompting techniques.
Report issue for preceding element
6.2 Prompt Engineering Case Study
Report issue for preceding element
Prompt engineering is emerging as an art that many people have begun to practice professionally, but the literature does not yet include detailed guidance on the process. As a first step in this direction, we present an annotated prompt engineering case study for a difficult real-world problem. This is not intended to be an empirical contribution in terms of actually solving the problem. Rather, it provides one illustration of how an experienced prompt engineer would approach a task like this, along with lessons learned.
Report issue for preceding element
6.2.1 Problem
Report issue for preceding element
Our illustrative problem involves detection of signal that is predictive of crisis-level suicide risk in text written by a potentially suicidal individual. Suicide is a severe problem worldwide, compounded, as are most mental health issues, by a desperate lack of mental health resources. In the United States, more than half the national population lives in federally defined mental heath provider shortage areas National Center for Health Workforce Analysis ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib200
) ; in addition, many mental health professionals lack core competencies in suicide prevention Cramer et al. ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib43
) . In 2021, 12.3M Americans thought seriously about suicide, with 1.7M actually making attempts resulting in over 48,000 deaths CDC ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib28
) . In the U.S., suicide was the second leading cause of death (after accidents) in people aged 10-14, 15-24, or 25-34 as of 2021 statistics, and it was the fifth leading cause of death in people aged 35–54 Garnett and Curtin ( 
2023
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib75
) .
Report issue for preceding element
Recent research suggests that there is significant value in assessments of potential suicidality that focus specifically on the identification of 
suicidal crisis
, i.e. the state of acute distress associated with a high risk of imminent suicidal behavior. However, validated assessments for diagnostic approaches such as Suicide Crisis Syndrome (SCS) Schuck et al. ( 
2019b
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib256
); Melzer et al. ( 
2024
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib187
) and Acute Suicidal Affective Disturbance (Rogers et al., 
2019
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib242
) require either personal clinical interactions or completion of self-report questionnaires that contain dozens of questions. The ability to accurately flag indicators of suicidal crisis in individuals' language could therefore have a large impact within the mental health ecosystem, not as a replacement for clinical judgment but as a way to complement existing practices (Resnik et al., 
2021
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib240
) .
Report issue for preceding element
As a starting point, we focus here on the most important predictive factor in Suicide Crisis Syndrome assessments, referred to in the literature as either 
frantic hopelessness
 or 
entrapment
, “a desire to escape from an unbearable situation, tied with the perception that all escape routes are blocked” (Melzer et al., 
2024
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib187
) . 
14
 
14
 14 The former term more explicitly emphasizes the frantic and desperate action required to escape an unbearable life situation. However, the term 
entrapment
 is briefer and used widely so we adopt it here. This characteristic of what an individual is experiencing is also central in other characterizations of mental processes that result in suicide.
Report issue for preceding element
6.2.2 The Dataset
Report issue for preceding element
We worked with a subset of data from the University of Maryland Reddit Suicidality Dataset Shing et al. ( 
2018
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib270
) , which is constructed from posts in r/SuicideWatch, a subreddit that offers peer support for anyone struggling with suicidal thoughts. Two coders trained on the recognition of the factors in Suicide Crisis Syndrome coded a set of 221 posts for presence or absence of entrapment, achieving solid inter-coder reliability (Krippendorff's alpha = 0.72 absent 0.72 =0.72 = 0.72 ).
Report issue for preceding element
6.2.3 The Process
Report issue for preceding element
An expert prompt engineer, who has authored a widely used guide on prompting Schulhoff ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib257
) , took on the task of using an LLM to identify entrapment in posts. 
15
 
15
 15 Disclosure: that expert is also the lead author of this paper. The prompt engineer was given a brief verbal and written summary of Suicide Crisis Syndrome and entrapment, along with 121 development posts and their positive/negative labels (where “positive” means entrapment is present), the other 100 labeled posts being reserved for testing. This limited information mirrors frequent real-life scenarios in which prompts are developed based on a task description and the data. More generally, it is consistent with a tendency in natural language processing and AI more generally to approach coding (annotation) as a labeling task without delving very deeply into the fact that the labels may, in fact, refer to nuanced and complex underlying social science constructs.
Report issue for preceding element
We documented the prompt engineering process in order to illustrate the way that an experienced prompt engineer goes about their work. The exercise proceeded through 47 recorded development steps, cumulatively about 20 hours of work. From a cold start with 0% performance (the prompt wouldn't return properly structured responses), performance was boosted to an F1 of 0.53, where that F1 is the harmonic mean of 0.86 precision and 0.38 recall. 
16
 
16
 16 Precision is also known as positive predictive value, and recall is also known as true positive rate or sensitivity. Although F1 is often used in computional system evaluations as a single figure of merit, we note that in this problem space its even weighting of precision and recall is probably not appropriate. We discuss this further below.
Report issue for preceding element

Figure 6.6: From the first prompt tried (Zero-Shot + Context) to the last (Anonymized Email), improvements in F1 score were hard to come by and and often involved testing multiple underperforming prompts before finding a performant one. Green lines show improvements over the current highest F1 score, while red lines show deteriorations. Report issue for preceding element
6.2.3.1 Dataset Exploration (2 steps)
Report issue for preceding element
The process began with the prompt engineer reviewing a description of entrapment (Figure 
6.7
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch6.F7
); this description had been used as a first-pass rubric for the human coders early in the coding process, noting, however, that they were familiar with SCS and knew it was neither a formal definition nor exhaustive. The prompt engineer then loaded the dataset into a Python notebook for data exploration purposes. He began by asking gpt-4-turbo-preview if it knew what entrapment was (Figure 
6.8
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch6.F8
), but found that the LLM's response was not similar to the description that had been given. In consequence, the prompt engineer included the Figure 
6.7
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch6.F7
 description of entrapment in all future prompts.
Report issue for preceding element
Entrapment: - Feeling like there is no exit - Feeling hopeless - Feeling like there is no way out - Feeling afraid that things will never be normal again - Feeling helpless to change - Feeling trapped - Feeling doomed - Feeling or thinking that things will never change - Feeling like there is no escape - Feeling like there are no good solutions to problems Report issue for preceding element Figure 6.7: The description of entrapment used by the prompt engineer Report issue for preceding element What is entrapment with respect to Suicide Crisis Syndrome? Report issue for preceding element Figure 6.8: Question asked to the LLM to determine whether its training data had provided relevant knowledge about entrapment (it had not). Report issue for preceding element
6.2.3.2 Getting a Label (8 steps)
Report issue for preceding element
As noted in Section 
6.1
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch6.S1
 with regard to the human_sexuality subset of MMLU, LLMs exhibit unpredictable and difficult to control behaviour in sensitive domains. For multiple steps in the prompt engineering process, the prompt engineer found that the LLM was giving mental health advice (e.g. Figure 
6.9
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch6.F9
) instead of labeling the input. This was addressed by switching to the GPT-4-32K model.
Report issue for preceding element
A take-away from this initial phase is that the “guard rails” associated with some large language models may interfere with the ability to make progress on a prompting task, and this could influence the choice of model for reasons other than the LLM's potential quality.
Report issue for preceding element
If you're in immediate danger of harming yourself, please contact emergency services or a crisis hotline in your area. They can provide immediate support and help ensure your safety. Report issue for preceding element Figure 6.9: A snippet from an output, which does not label the data point, but rather attempts to provide mental health support to the user. Such outputs are often five times as long as this snippet. Report issue for preceding element
6.2.3.3 Prompting Techniques (32 steps)
Report issue for preceding element
The prompt engineer then spent the majority of his time improving the prompting technique being used. This included techniques such as Few-Shot, Chain-of-Thought, AutoCoT, Contrastive CoT, and multiple answer extraction techniques. We report statistics for the first runs of these techniques; F1 scores could change by as much as 0.04 upon subsequent runs, even with temperature and top p set to zero. 
17
 
17
 17 Temperature and top-p are configuration hyperparameters that control randomness of the output Schulhoff ( 
2022
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#bib.bib257
) .
Report issue for preceding element
Zero-Shot + Context
Report issue for preceding element
was the first technique evaluated (Figure 
6.10
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch6.F10
), using the description in Figure 
6.7
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch6.F7
. Notice the word 
definition
 in the prompt, although Figure 
6.7
https://arxiv.org/html/2406.06608v6#Ch6.F7
 is not a formal definition.
Report issue for preceding element
In order to obtain a final response from the LLM to use in calculating performance metrics, it was necessary to extract a label from the LLM output. The prompt engineer tested two extractors, one that checks if the output is exactly "Yes" or "No", and another which just checks if those words match the first few characters of the output. The latter had better performance, and it is used for the rest of this section until we reach CoT. This approach obtained 0.40 F1, 1.0 recall, and 0.25 precision, evaluated on all samples from the training/development since no samples had been used as exemplars.
